By now, basketball fans are surely aware that Ernie Johnson is leaving Inside the NBA after the 2026-2027 NBA season. Johnson made the announcement at the end of October, and fans and celebrities alike have since been honoring him. One of the most emotional tributes, though, came from Johnson’s longtime co-hosts, Charles Barkley, who spoke about the news during an episode of the talk show. I was already in my feelings over the on-air tribute but, now, that’s amped up a bit since I’ve heard Johnson’s first-hand account.

Johnson initially revealed his news in a social media video that eventually went viral on October 28, and he spoke about it again at the top of Inside’s broadcast that evening. Following that opening, Johnson teed up the show, as per usual, by introducing co-hosts Barkley, Kenny Smith and Shaquille O’Neal. When Johnson threw it to Barkley, however, he was misty-eyed and momentarily at a loss for words. Johnson admitted on The Dan LeBatard Show (as seen on YouTube) that he was surprised, saying:

I was concerned, at first, because the camera was on him and he wasn't saying anything, and he had to kind of — like he was struggling and I was like, 'Oh, what's going on here?' The moments don't get much better than that for me when I think about the last few days. Because look, I've said it before, you guys have heard me, we love each other. We've been together for so long. We would do anything for one another.

That night, when the camera was on him, the usually humorous Barkley took a few seconds to compose himself. He eventually recovered, simply saying, “That was tough,” in a low and solemn voice. “Sir Charles” then proceeded to give a truly warm tribute to Johnson, which beautifully summed up the 26 years they’ve worked together. Check out Barkley’s tribute, and those of Smith and O’Neal by watching the video below:

Months ago, Charles Barkley claimed he would retire after this season and, in the process of doing so, he let slip that Johnson was doing the same. Barkley voiced his desire for both him and his co-host to step away from the show together. Johnson didn’t immediately confirm his plans for weeks, leaving much uncertainty. In the video, Johnson explained that he wanted to step away from the show as a way of slowing down a bit in order to spend some time with his family and focus on other ventures. Johnson also said this of his on-air tribute:

It was emotional for me. I mean, I was holding it together all day long and it was like, ‘Oh, man. I'm watching Chuck struggle with this.’ But that just speaks to the relationship we all have.

Charles Barkley Shares More Thoughts On Retiring From Inside The NBA, And I’m Starting To Believe He’ll Do It This Time

Johnson began hosting Inside back in the fall of 1990 and, under his leadership, the show became a must-watch program for basketball devotees like myself. The “Godfather” has been present for the series’ biggest moments, including its move from TNT to ESPN last season. Considering the veteran broadcaster’s long tenure with the program, the notion of him no longer hosting is somewhat hard to think about. What’s more is that there’s still the question of whether Barkley will truly retire (this time) as he declared.

Whatever the case, ESPN is reportedly committed to Inside the NBA regardless of Ernie Johnson and Charles Barkley’s plans. I’m not sure I’m ready to think about the prospect of the show being with two of its key members. For now, though, I’m just relishing this final season with the core four and hoping it produces even more memorable moments like Barkley’s sentimental tribute.

NASA Awards Moon Base Transportation, Technology, Science Projects

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NASA announced a suite of awards and mission updates on Saturday to expand the agency’s Moon Base exploration, science, and infrastructure capabilities. The investments will advance commercial robotic deliveries, a nuclear-powered rover named PROMISE, launch and orbital transfer services, and surface infrastructure as the agency works with American industry to establish a sustained presence on the Moon’s South Pole.

The agency shared the news at the MAX POWER aerospace expo and air show at NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

“We are building the Moon Base mission by mission,” said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman. “Today’s announcements expand nearly every part of that campaign, using the playbook that carried NASA through its greatest eras: fly often, learn, apply those lessons, and get more better and more capable with every mission. When our astronauts return to the Moon, they will arrive with the first elements of the Moon Base already waiting for them on the surface.”

“In the eight months since Ignition, we’ve made incredible strides in accomplishing the near impossible: establishing a permanent outpost on the lunar surface,” said Carlos García-Galán, manager, Moon Base Program. “We’ve consolidated numerous efforts into a streamlined program, evolving our human exploration architecture into an iterative and sustained campaign for Moon Base technology development and implementation. Most importantly, we’ve taken action, awarding new contracts and forging new partnerships to accelerate development.”

To strengthen the cargo lander network needed to support the Moon Base, NASA selected eight companies for the next phase of its CLPS (Commercial Lunar Payload Services) initiative. The companies are eligible to compete for future lunar surface delivery missions under the new CLPS 2 contract:

Under the CLPS 2 framework, the companies will provide lunar delivery services, including managing, building, and operating landers that transport NASA science, technology, and infrastructure payloads to the Moon.

The CLPS 2 framework is a 10-year, multiple-award, indefinite-delivery/indefinite-quantity master contract with a combined maximum value of $10 billion. NASA increased the value of the current CLPS 1.0 contract from $2.6 billion to $4.2 billion to support initial Phase One Moon Base objectives. The original CLPS contract will remain in place through November 2028, with an additional five-year period to complete awarded lunar deliveries through November 2033.

NASA also announced it is moving forward with development of PROMISE (Polar Rover for Observation, Mapping, and In-Situ Exploration). Carrying science instruments and technology demonstrations, the six-wheeled rover will land at the Moon’s South Pole to characterize the surface and subsurface, assess resource potential, and provide data to enable landing site selection, as well as future infrastructure and exploration activities. The nuclear-powered rover can operate during long lunar nights and within permanently shadowed regions thought to harbor ice, which is crucial for a sustained human presence on the Moon.

The agency’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California, a division of Caltech, will manage the mission and build the rover from a combination of spare flight components and engineering test models for NASA’s Curiosity and Perseverance Mars rovers. Using proven hardware, software, and Mars rover operation approaches significantly reduces cost and enables integration and testing of the complex PROMISE rover on a shorter timeline. NASA has assessed the compatibility of the hardware with the lunar environment and determined it can tolerate the harsh conditions found near the Moon’s South Pole.

The PROMISE rover will carry a drill to extract regolith, or lunar dust, to characterize ice and other resources from approximately 3 feet (1 meter) below the surface, spectrometers for analyzing the chemical composition of surface material, temperature and radiation sensors, and visible-light cameras. It will demonstrate a next-generation, radiation-tolerant computing system, NASA’s High Performance Spaceflight Computing, and advanced autonomy sensors and algorithms. NASA is targeting late 2028 to launch PROMISE and planning an 18-month mission for the rover.

NASA awarded Blue Origin a $94 million base contract, with an option period worth an additional $140 million based on performance to deliver PROMISE to the lunar South Pole. The award expands the task order awarded in May 2026 to deliver two lunar terrain vehicles to the Moon. Blue Origin will use its Mark 1 lander to deliver and deploy the rover as part of the Moon Base’s first phase.

Delivering Moon Base infrastructure will require a robust transportation network between Earth and the Moon. Orbital Transfer Vehicles (OTVs) act like long-distance freight trucks, carrying large loads across space. Lunar landers handle the final delivery, bringing supplies, equipment, and infrastructure safely down to the Moon’s surface.

To demonstrate those capabilities, NASA awarded six task orders as part of the agency’s Venture-Class Acquisition of Dedicated and Rideshare (VADR) launch services contract. The awards were given to Arrow Science and Technology, Blue Origin, and Impulse Space to integrate and transport Moon Base cargo.

Three mission types will allow NASA flexibility to fly science and technology demonstrations when they are ready.

The first will include missions to reduce risks of future long-term human spaceflight for lunar exploration. The missions will verify that communication and navigation methods work properly during the journey from Earth orbit to the Moon, and OTV capability to support delivery of a CLPS lunar lander to a desired orbit. This mission type will deliver the CAPSTONE 02 spacecraft to deep space to test proximity maneuvers, navigation, and communications around the Moon.

The second category of missions will prepare the lunar South Pole for human exploration by sending a group of highly mobile drones to survey the surface before astronauts arrive, enabling safer, more informed human missions while demonstrating advanced robotic capabilities. Such missions will include the agency’s MoonFall drones, designed to scout areas beyond the reach of traditional rovers, as well as delivery of additional Moon Base assets necessary to support Phase One objectives.

To reduce risk for future missions, the third mission type will test navigation technologies and demonstrate operations in multiple cislunar orbits, between Earth and the Moon. This mission type will demonstrate commercial orbital transfer capabilities intended to increase the amount of cargo that future landers can deliver to the lunar surface.

The task orders have a value of approximately $1.3 billion combined and fall under the 10-year VADR indefinite-delivery/indefinite-quantity contract, which has a maximum total value of $5 billion across all contracts. The VADR contracts are managed by NASA’s Launch Services Program Office at the agency’s Kennedy Space Center as part of the agency’s Commercial Access to Space Program. By procuring multiple launch and in-space transportation services together, NASA provides industry with a more predictable set of mission opportunities while gaining access to a broader range of commercial capabilities for future lunar exploration.

Some missions will launch aboard Blue Origin’s New Glenn, Relativity Space’s Terran R, and SpaceX’s Falcon 9 rockets. In-space transportation spacecraft for these missions will be provided by Blue Origin, Firefly Aerospace, Impulse Space, and Quantum Space.

Complementing those in-space transportation demonstrations, NASA selected SpaceX for the Cislunar Communications Deployment Demonstration under an existing lunar logistics contract. Through a Specialized Delivery Mission task order, SpaceX will launch communications satellites aboard a Starship to cislunar space in 2028. The demonstration will help inform the development of future lunar communications networks that keep astronauts, mission controllers, and the Moon Base connected.

To extend infrastructure capabilities on the lunar surface, NASA plans to purchase commercial components for a Mini-Communications and Solar Tower (miniCAST), including solar arrays, batteries, and wireless power-transfer hardware. The agency awarded Honeybee Robotics, a Blue Origin company, for energy storage capability, Redwire for power generation, and Yank Technologies, Inc., for wireless power transfer in support of miniCAST.

The miniCAST will combine power and communications capabilities to support future Moon Base activities. Engineers at NASA’s Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville, Alabama, will assemble and test the systems before they are delivered to the lunar surface.

NASA expects work to begin within months, and the initial effort will involve components rather than a major integrated system. The agency also plans a separate solicitation for fully integrated power systems, which is anticipated in early 2027.

NASA intends to release a directed call under its Next Space Technologies for Exploration Partnerships, or NextSTEP-3 B, solicitation to help close architecture-driven, high-priority data gaps for Moon Base development. The effort will help NASA gather information it needs to plan future missions, reduce risk, and support technologies and infrastructure for sustained operations on the Moon’s South Pole. The work could include small spacecraft missions, payloads on future Moon Base flights, or the purchase of data collected by commercial partners.

As part of a Golden Age of innovation and exploration, NASA will send Artemis astronauts on increasingly difficult missions to the lunar South Pole to explore more of the Moon than ever before. In addition to providing economic benefits and advancing scientific discovery through its Moon missions, NASA will establish an enduring human presence on the lunar surface and build on this foundation for the first crewed mission to Mars.

To learn more about the Moon Base, visit:

George Alderman / James GannonHeadquarters, Washington202-358-1600george.a.alderman@nasa.gov / james.h.gannon@nasa.gov

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

The 4 Largest Air Force Bases By Bomber Fleet Presence

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The United States is one of only three nations in the world that possess large, strategic bomber aircraft in its armed forces. The US Air Force operates the second-largest strategic strike fleet of 130 bombers under Air Force Global Strike Command, which, despite being numerically smaller than the Chinese Air Force fleet, is technologically far superior. The impressive fleet is home-based at a few different installations across the US.

This allows it to distribute forces for rapid deployment, centralized maintenance and training, as well as dispersion that reduces the vulnerability of AFGSC to a single, large-scale attack. This review will focus on the four largest airbases, which not only have the highest concentration of planes but also feature unique facilities that specialize in supporting different airframes at each one. The future of strategic strike within the USAF is following a 'high-low' fleet philosophy.

The Air Force is pursuing this with its fighter jets as well. The incoming Northrop Grumman B-21 Raider will replace both the B-2 Spirit and the Rockwell B-1 Lancer as the premier bomber platform. The bases of the bomber fleet are following in step with the transition, which marks a watershed moment in American military history.

Following a host of upgrades, the new Boeing B-52J Stratofortress variant is being positioned as a 'flying arsenal ship.' While the B-21 Raider handles the stealthy penetration, Barksdale's B-52Js will follow behind, launching massive volleys of standoff missiles from outside enemy air defenses. As the tactics and strategy of the Air Force evolve, so too are the biggest airbases from which these incredible warbirds fly.

Whiteman Air Force Base in Missouri is the world’s only base for the B-2 Spirit. Whiteman hosts the entire active fleet of 19 B-2 bombers. These aircraft are operated by the 509th Bomb Wing (active duty) and the 131st Bomb Wing (Missouri Air National Guard). Whiteman has also been officially selected as the second operational base for the next-generation B-21 Raider stealth bomber.

Before becoming a bomber hub, Whiteman was a major Minuteman II Intercontinental Ballistic Missile base. The base maintained over 150 missile silos until all were deactivated in the nuclear disarmament and non-proliferation efforts that followed the collapse of the USSR. The deactivation was driven by the 1991 Strategic Arms Reduction Treaty.

As the missile mission faded, Whiteman was selected as the sole operational base for the B-2. Air & Space Forces Magazine recounted these estimates from an Air Force report on the projected changes at Whiteman to accommodate the new B-21 fleet:

“There would be an increase of 1,021 individuals at Whiteman AFB from 19,408 to 20,429 after all B-21 mission individuals have arrived and all B-2 mission individuals have departed. The number of airfield operations flown per year would increase by 1,980, from 29,771 (…) to 31,751 by the time all the new aircraft arrived. There would be a need for increased base housing as a result."

The first operational B-2, named the 'Spirit of Missouri,' arrived at Whiteman in 1993. The 509th Bomb Wing, which was the same unit that dropped both atomic bombs in the Second World War, moved to the base to begin training on the world's first stealth bomber. The wing achieved Initial Operational Capability in 1997. The B-2 saw its first combat use in 1999 during Operation Allied Force, with crews flying 30-hour nonstop missions from Missouri to Kosovo.

Ellsworth Air Force Base, located near Rapid City, South Dakota, is the current home of the 28th Bomb Wing and is set to become the first operational base of the B-21 Raider. Ellsworth hosts two of the Air Force's three active B-1B Lancer squadrons: the 34th Bomb Squadron (Thunderbirds) and the 37th Bomb Squadron (Tigers). Ellsworth oversees the PRTC, the largest training airspace in the continental United States, spanning four states.

This area allows bombers to practice low-level maneuvers and large-force exercises. The transition at Ellsworth represents the most significant shift in American airpower in decades. The first operational B-21 aircraft are scheduled to arrive in 2027. During the Cold War, the base was the heart of Strategic Air Command, maintaining a dual-leg mission that included both heavy bombers and 150 Minuteman II ICBM silos.

The base is undergoing a massive $1.5 billion+ transformation to accommodate the new stealth armada. Dale White, the Director of Critical Major Weapon Systems at the Pentagon, said in a statement reported by Stripes on February 24 that “we are responsibly accelerating delivery of a critical, combat-effective capability."

The USAF also recently announced a 25% increase in B-21 production capacity. The remaining fleet of B-1B Lancerswill begin retiring in stages to make way for the incoming next-generation bombers. Nine aircraft are slated for divestment in 2028, followed by 10 more in 2029, and the entire B-1 fleet is expected to be retired by 2036.

Dyess Air Force Base in Abilene, Texas, remains one of the most critical strike and airlift hubs in the United States. The 7th Bomb Wing operates approximately 33 B-1B Lancer aircraft. Dyess is the sole formal training unit for all B-1B aircrews in the USAF, managed by the 28th Bomb Squadron. Dyess has also been chosen as the third main operating base for the new B-21 stealth bomber. Per The War Zone, Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink said about B-21's the production acceleration that:

“The B-21 is foundational to our long-range strike capability and to credible deterrence. Accelerating production capacity now ensures we deliver operational capability to combatant commanders faster, strengthening our ability to outpace, deter, and, if necessary, defeat emerging threats. This is disciplined execution at the speed the security environment demands.”

The arrival of the B-21 will bring nuclear weapons capability back to Dyess for the first time since the 1990s. Work officially began last year on the first B-21 facilities, and 2026 is slated for groundbreaking on a mission planning facility that will be the first major facility built exclusively for the Raider. These revolutionary aircraft will share the tarmac with another major tenant at Dyess, the 317th Airlift Wing. The wing is the largest Lockheed Martin C-130J Super Hercules unit in the world.

Dyess supports approximately 15,000 people, including over 4,100 active-duty aviators and their families. It was the first Department of Defense installation to be powered entirely by renewable wind energy. The base is the largest employer in the Abilene area, contributing over $3.6 billion annually to the Texas economy. Dyess is currently undergoing more than 20 major infrastructure projects designed to support the unique requirements of a 6th-Gen stealth bomber.

This aging bomber continues to adapt, proving its resilience in a changing world.

The 2nd Bomb Wing operates approximately 44 B-52s at Barksdale AFB. The 307th Bomb Wing, an Air Force Reserve unit, also operates an additional 18 to 20 B-52Hs. The base also serves as the permanent headquarters for the AFGSC, which oversees all B-1, B-2, B-52, and B-21 wings, as well as the nation’s three ICBM wings.

The legendary 'Mighty Eighth' Air Force is headquartered at Barksdale and at the forefront of the B-52J upgrade. This includes new Rolls-Royce engines and digital cockpit systems designed to keep the B-52 flying until the 2050s. While Barksdale will remain the primary B-52 hub, it is also deeply involved in the rollout of the B-21 Raider, as the headquarters for AFGSC.

The B-52J will feature the AN/APG-79 AESA radar, which wields the same technology found in the F/A-18 Super Hornet carrier-based, multirole fighter jet. This allows for vastly superior target tracking, mapping, and electronic warfare capabilities. The aircraft's cockpit is being digitized to allow the crew to be reduced from five members to four on some missions.

Michael Maginness, the 2nd Bomb Wing's Commander, told Air & Space Forces Magazine that “the B-52 has been and will continue to be a very strong message of US support for allies and deterrence." He explained that the B-52 does so "just by flying through their airspace or putting a jet down on a runway in a friendly nation.” On this front, Joshua Crowe, a B-52 Engine Maintainer, explained that:

“With the new ones, they’re proposed to have enclosed systems, so you don’t have to spend more time on the back-end servicing. And the longevity of them: they’re supposed to be able to sit on the wing longer in between changes, if not, maybe not even change at all for the lifetime of it. Essentially, we’re going to be able to extend our missions longer and reduce our touch time on the ground so that we can free up those technicians to do other things.”

The most visible change is the replacement of the 1960s-era Pratt & Whitney engines with the Rolls-Royce F130. These engines provide 30% better fuel efficiency, increased range, and significantly reduced maintenance costs. The base is also upgrading to handle engine testing alongside the introduction of the Long-Range Stand-Off cruise missile for the B-52.

What does NAT type mean for online gaming?

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NAT type isn’t always an issue, and depends on how your network is configured.

Inside an online game's settings or on your console you may have seen the NAT Type field and wondered what it meant. In some cases, you might even get a warning that your NAT type is strict and that matchmaking will be affected.

In many cases, this is more informational than anything you need to act on. If your game is working fine in matchmaking and voice chat, then adjustments shouldn't be necessary. But if your network is configured poorly for gaming and you keep getting disconnected or seeing errors, you should make some changes to avoid performance issues. Let's look over what NAT type means, how it affects you and what you can do to adjust it.

NAT stands for network address translation and is a key part of how the internet has worked for some time. IPv4, the original scheme for IP addresses, only has enough room for about four billion unique numbers. That was plenty when the standard was developed in the '80s but it quickly became stretched once everyone got online and started adding dozens of smart devices.

ISPs came up with NAT as a solution. With this, each home has one public IP address and its router translates incoming and outgoing traffic so all devices within it work under a single public IP. It's not a significant roadblock to most network traffic, but for real-time applications like gaming small bits of inconsistency and these layers of translation can have a big effect.

The newer version of the internet protocol, IPv6, has enough possible IP addresses for every grain of sand on Earth to have billions of their own. It thus doesn't require NAT because every device can have its own IP address.

Many modern devices support IPv6, but the transition hasn't fully completed yet. Some devices work in a hybrid mode where they can take advantage of IPv6 but fall back to IPv4 if there are issues or another device along the chain isn't compatible.

NAT type in a game explains how well your device can communicate with the wider internet. On Xbox and PC, you'll see three types: Open, Moderate and Strict. On PlayStation, these are respectively known as Type 1, Type 2 and Type 3. The Nintendo Switch uses A, B and C, with D for connections that are heavily restricted and F if your console can't connect at all. NAT types are not an official network specification, which is why they vary across platforms.

An Open NAT means your gaming console or PC is directly connected to the internet and has no restrictions on what it can communicate with. (As explained above, this isn't actually NAT, but games still designate it as such.) Moderate indicates that your system is behind a router for its internet connection but is still able to communicate as needed. Finally, Strict means your system has necessary ports blocked and can't communicate normally.

Although Open sounds like it should be the goal, that's not really the case. Having your console or PC directly connected to the internet is impractical, as it would require you to connect your machine to your modem, preventing you from using a router to get every other device online unless you used a network switch. Importantly, it would also be insecure since you wouldn't have the firewall or other security features of your router protecting your network traffic.

With an Open NAT type, you can connect to anyone else; and with Moderate you can connect to other Moderate or Open players. Strict is the least compatible, as you can only play with those who have an Open NAT. Usually, someone with an Open NAT is chosen as the game host, while having a Strict NAT means you're more likely to see matchmaking errors, won't be able to join friends and similar.

Your experience is most affected by NAT type in games with peer-to-peer (P2P) connections rather than dedicated servers provided by the developer. However, even in games with their own servers, elements like in-game voice chat, custom lobbies, co-op campaigns and similar might still rely on P2P connections.

NAT type doesn't change your network speed or ping; it only affects who your console can talk to. Improving your router's download speeds is another matter.

To check your NAT type on PS5, go to Settings > Network > Connection Status > Test Internet Connection, and there you'll be able to see your NAT type. On Xbox Series X|S, go to Settings > General > Network Settings and check under Current Network Status. On a Switch or Switch 2, go to Settings > Internet > Test Connection. Some games will also show your NAT in their network settings or on the multiplayer main menu.

If your NAT type is Open/Type 1/A or Moderate/Type 2/B, you're in good shape for multiplayer games. In case you see a worse rating, you can make a few adjustments on your network, but there isn't a simple toggle to flip.

First, make sure you aren't using two routers. This creates a problem called double NAT where there are two layers of address translation happening. If you have a router connected to your ISP's gateway, putting the gateway in bridge mode will prevent double NAT. Anyone who uses a second router as an access point should put it in AP mode so it's not doubling up on router functionality.

Aside from that, the most common fix for a Strict NAT type is enabling UPnP (Universal Plug and Play) on your router. NAT type problems occur because games and online services can't communicate with your console over the relevant ports. Fixing this involves logging into your router and forwarding the relevant ports to your console or gaming PC to unblock relevant communication from the outside, but doing so for every game is inconvenient.

When UPnP is enabled, any device on your network can request ports that it needs forwarded from your router and they will be granted. This is convenient but also creates security risks. If someone malicious gained access to your network, they could abuse UPnP to request ports be opened that give them more control. You can try a UPnP test to see if your network listens for UPnP requests outside your home LAN; if so, it's even more dangerous. Especially if you don't play a lot of different games, spending a bit of time port forwarding is probably worth the security benefit. Port Forward is a good resource for getting started.

Failing all this, the problem can be caused by CGNAT, or carrier-grade NAT. This is when ISPs apply NAT to entire areas that they service, meaning the public IP address of your router goes through its own layer of translation by your ISP. Using IPv6 is a workaround for this, though Xbox is the only console that uses it fully. The PS5 and Switch 2 support IPv6 but require IPv4 alongside it. If enabling IPv6 on your router isn't enough, try contacting your ISP and requesting a dedicated public IP.

Chromagraph Camera is exactly the camera app I’ve been looking for on iPhone

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Last week, movie director Rian Johnson announced a surprise new camera app for iPhone that reimagines what a smartphone photo should look like. Many people would agree that Apple’s iPhone photos are a tad bit overprocessed.

iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo introduced a new film texture option, but in my view, it’s a bit too exaggerated. Chromagraph Camera, on the other hand, feels pretty perfect.

Attempts have been made to improve iPhone photos for years, but they all feel a little bit too heavy, in my view. Most camera apps for iPhone require you to dial settings in. While that can be appropriate for certain settings, I ultimately want my iPhone to just be a point and shoot experience. If I wanted the perfect photo, I’d be using my camera.

That said, iPhone photos just don’t look super excellent out of the box. It’s not a limitation of hardware. If you take a RAW iPhone photo and edit it yourself, you can actually get surprisingly good results. Apple’s camera app just has poor defaults.

When Apple introduced the film grain feature on iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo, I was actually quite interested in it. It was certainly my favorite camera feature of the phone when I briefly tried out the iPhone 18 Pro. For some reason though, the film look just feels a bit exaggerated on iPhone Duo.

Apps like Adobe’s Project Indigo have gotten really close in terms of look, but the fact that there’s so much time spent post-processing means it’s often inconvenient. I’ve also had a few instances of losing photos because the app closed in the background while it was still processing the photo.

When Chromagraph Camera was announced, I was quite interested. I paid the $12.99, and I’ve been happy with the results. I tweeted some of my sample photos when I just got the app, and it’s been well worth it.

$12.99 may be a higher starting price, but if you really want an iPhone camera app that just takes good looking photos without a lot of hassle, it works really well.

Why your screen sharing drops to 5 fps instead of lowering resolution

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When a screen share gets choppy, most people assume the network is bad. Sometimes it is. But there is a quieter failure mode where the network is fine, the encoder is fine, and the picture still collapses to single-digit frame rates while resolution never moves. The user ends up with the worst of both worlds: a soft frame that also stutters.

The reason is a default you probably never set: which dimension the pipeline is allowed to sacrifice under pressure.

WebRTC exposes degradationPreference on a sender, with three values: maintain-resolution, maintain-framerate, and balanced. The names suggest a symmetric trade-off. The behavior is not symmetric in practice.

For screen content, many stacks lean toward preserving resolution, because text and tables are unreadable if you halve the dimensions. That reasoning is sound for a still image. Video is not a still image. When the encoder is told to keep 3024×1964 and the bitrate budget tightens, it cannot keep quality by magic. It keeps the pixel count, spends fewer bits per frame, and the result is visibly soft. At the same time, the pacing logic has to shed something, and what it sheds is frame rate.

So "maintain resolution" does not actually protect legibility. It protects a number. What the viewer sees is a soft, low-frame-rate stream that is neither sharp nor fluid.

We ran an end-to-end capture of a 3024×1964 window, H.264 through the hardware encoder (VideoToolbox), sender and viewer on the same subnet.

Read those together. The encoder is not struggling — 12.3 ms per frame leaves headroom. No frames were dropped at the encoder. The bandwidth estimator never once asked the pipeline to constrain itself. And yet the delivered frame rate bottomed out at 3.

That is the important part: adaptation never engaged. The 3 fps was not the controller choosing a lower frame rate under a bandwidth limit. It was the absence of any decision. Resolution stayed at full size the entire run because nothing touched it, and frame rate fell for reasons upstream of the decision the controller makes.

If you only watch average frame rate, this looks like "bad network." It is not. It is a pipeline with no owner for the resolution-versus-frame-rate choice.

None of these are solved by a preference flag. A preference says what to do if the engine decides to act. It does not make the engine act, and it does not remove capture-side load.

Do not inherit it. Decide, per session, what the product needs:

For screen sharing where motion and responsiveness matter, maintain-framerate is often the better default — a soft frame that keeps updating is easier to follow than a sharp frame that freezes. For a document being read line by line, the opposite may be true. Either way, the choice is a product decision, not something to leave to the engine.

Instead of waiting for adaptation, step resolution yourself on a schedule tied to observed conditions:

Call applyConstraints with a reduced width/height, or use scaleResolutionDownBy on the sender. The point is that each step is a real, bounded reduction that lowers encode cost and queue depth — not a preference the engine may ignore.

The cheapest fix is upstream: do not capture at native size and hope the encoder manages it. Cap the capture dimensions to what the viewer can actually resolve. A 3024-pixel-wide frame on a 1440-pixel viewer is mostly wasted bits, and it makes every downstream stage more expensive. The pipeline gets easier at every stage when the source is already the size you intend to send.

The uncomfortable summary: when the engine keeps resolution and drops frames, it is making a choice you did not make. That choice is defensible for a photograph and wrong for a live screen. A number that preserves legibility at 3 fps is an illusion — a sharp frame no one can read because it never changes.

Instrument the frame-rate distribution, not the average. Watch whether limit ever leaves none. If it never does, adaptation is not saving you, and the resolution ladder is yours to build.

I also made a free calculator for this: Screen Sharing Latency & Bandwidth Calculator — plug in resolution, frame rate, codec, and available bandwidth to see where the budget breaks.

Why an Agent Hardcodes Environment Config in Source

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 7 • Originally published at powerduck.com

Quick answer: Environment config belongs outside the source because it changes between development, staging, and production while the code stays the same. When an agent writes a URL, bucket name, or connection string as a literal to make the current run pass, it bakes one environment's values into every deploy. Read environment-specific values from a single config layer, validate them at startup, and keep secrets out of the repository.

A literal is the shortest path to a green run. The agent needs a value to proceed, the environment is not defined, and typing it inline removes the friction immediately. Training data is full of examples with hardcoded endpoints, and unless the task explicitly asks for externalized config, the agent optimizes for working code on this machine rather than portable code on the next.

The failure is silent at first. It works on the agent's machine because that literal happens to match the local environment. It breaks when the same code points at a development database from production, when a host name changes and requires a full rebuild, or when a committed credential has to be rotated by editing source.

Not every literal is a problem. Values that never change between environments, like a fixed page size or a well-known constant, can stay in code. The dividing line is whether the value differs by environment or deployment. For how this fits the broader work of running coding agents well, see the AI coding agent guide.

Use one loading point. Read the environment once, in a single module, and expose typed values to the rest of the app instead of reading process variables ad hoc across the codebase. That gives you one place to audit and one place to fail.

Provide safe defaults for non-sensitive values and let the environment override them, but never guess a missing required value. Validate that every required variable is present and well formed at startup, and fail fast with a clear message before the app handles traffic. Keep secrets in a secret manager rather than a committed .env file; a local .env, listed in .gitignore, works for development as long as the required variables are documented.

Centralizing reads also makes the configuration discoverable. When every variable enters through one module, you can list the full set of environment dependencies in a single place and confirm nothing reads a variable the startup check does not know about. Scattered reads hide unused and missing variables, and they make a secret harder to locate because it could be referenced anywhere in the codebase.

Keep defaults honest. A default works for a non-critical value with a safe fallback, but it should never point at a real resource. If a missing production variable silently falls back to a development endpoint, the problem stays hidden until the wrong data is written. Required values should raise an error rather than inherit a convenient default.

For the boundary with a related failure, committing a machine-specific absolute path is a separate portability problem; here the issue is values that legitimately change between environments. Powerduck keeps environment-specific values in scoped variables rather than literals, with globals, collection, environment, and local scopes, and when no base URL is available it asks for one instead of guessing, which is the same fail-fast rule applied to request configuration.

Is a .env file enough? For local development, yes, as long as it is not committed and secrets are excluded. In production, prefer a managed secret store and inject values through the platform.

Should I externalize every constant? No. Move only values that change across environments or deployments. Externalizing stable constants adds indirection with no benefit.

What if the agent keeps hardcoding values? Make externalization part of the task and the review. Require the config layer up front and reject any diff that introduces an environment-specific literal.

Code is built once and deployed many times; environment config is the part that changes between those deployments. Keep the two separate, load config through one validated entry point, fail loudly when a required value is missing, and never commit a secret. A run that passes on a hardcoded value is only passing for one environment.

A filterable table that still works with JavaScript off

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 7 • Edited on Nov 7 • AI-assisted

I made a small page for health.dailyappslab.com that filters 259 US fast-food menu items by protein and calories. The filtering is the easy part. What I cared about was that the page still shows everything when the script doesn't run.

The data is one TypeScript file. Each item has a chain, a name, calories, protein and a few optional fields like sodium. Each chain entry also stores the nutrition page the numbers came from and the date I read it, so the source sits next to the data instead of in my memory.

Astro renders every item as a table row at build time. Each row carries its values as data attributes:

"Protein per 100 calories" is computed once in the data file, not in the browser:

With JavaScript off, you get the full table, already sorted by that number. Crawlers that don't run scripts see the same page.

The script only hides and reorders rows. It reads the form, filters the rows by their data attributes, sorts what's left, and sets hidden on the rest:

There is no framework on the page and no fetch call. The table is already in the DOM, so the script only moves rows around.

The filters also live in the URL. After each change the script writes them with history.replaceState and URLSearchParams, and on load it reads them back, so a filtered list can be shared as a link. The form is a plain GET form to the same path, which makes it behave sensibly as markup. Since the site is static, though, the server ignores the query string and only the script acts on it.

Two limits. The numbers are a snapshot: I copied them from each chain's own US nutrition page on the dates shown in the page, so they will drift when menus change. And they are US menus only. All 259 rows were checked against the article tables they came from before the page went live. If the data grows, a script that runs that comparison on every build is what I'd add first.

If you want to see it run, the page is here: https://health.dailyappslab.com/macroscout/protein-finder/. It's free and needs no signup.

Disclosure: this is a page on my own site, built at Daily Apps Lab, and I used AI tools to help draft this post.

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sous les yeux de Christian Lopez, qui a donné le coup d'envoi fictif de cette rencontre entre ses deux anciens clubs, la réserve s'est imposée (2-1) ce samedi sur le fil et sur le terrain du Cannet-Rocheville (Alpes Maritimes) grâce à renversant doublé de Lamine Sonko (photo).

Après un premier quart d'heure plutôt équilibré, l'ASSE a pris l'ascendant mais n'a pas su ouvrir le score malgré deux grosses occasions de son très remuant ailier gauche Etienne Mendy et une tentative de Mamadou Konté sauvée in extremis sur sa ligne par un défenseur.

Sainté a concédé l'ouverture du score à la 56e minute suite à un 6 mètres joué trop vite suivi d'une perte de balle. Les Maralpins ont bien joué le coup sur cette action qui semblait quand même entachée d'une légère faute sur Mamadou Konté.

Boostés par l'apport de leurs entrants, les Verts ont égalisé à la 86e minute sur une ouverture de leur latéral droit Tom Teillol pour Lamine Sonko, qui a résisté au retour de deux défenseurs avant de mettre un petit piqué pour tromper le mal prénommé mais bien nommé Victor Battu.

A la 92e minute, Nathan Kasia a perdu le ballon mais s'est bagarré pour le récupérer et lancer Noah Moulin, dont le centre tendu a été repris victorieusement par le supersub Lamine Sonko. Ce succès permet aux Verts de monter provisoirement à la 2e place du classement, derrière le solide leader Berre.

La réaction de Sylvain Gibert : "On a su faire la différence à la fin du match mais on aurait mérité de mener au score à la pause car la dernière demi-heure de la première période a été très bonne. On a manqué d'efficacité devant le but, c'est donc notre adversaire qui a ouvert le score. Mais on est reparti de l'avant, on a insisté et au final on a été récompensé. C'est la victoire de tout un groupe, les entrants notamment nous ont apporté l'énergie nécessaire pour renverser ce match. Les joueus étaient venus ici pour gagner et ils se sont donnés les moyens d'y parvenir. Il faut les féliciter pour ça. On est content d'avoir décroché ce succès sous les yeux de Christian Lopez, qui nous a fait l'honneur d'être présent et d'échanger avec nous à la fin du match. C'était top car c'est un joueur que j'ai cité la saison dernière dans mes causeries. Je trouve qu'il représente les valeurs du club, cette capacité à ne jamais renoncer, comme l'illustre son mythique intervention sur Oleg Blokhine contre Kiev."

"This trailer is my way of saying goodbye": Have a listen to the Mass Effect 5 score that could have been, courtesy of one of the original games' composers

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

By this point I'm probably safe in assuming that you all know EA are shutting down BioWare, and that Mass Effect 5 has been cancelled in turn. You may have also seen that an internal trailer for Mass Effect 5, or Universe as it was seemingly going to be called, was leaked not long after the news broke, and that Sam Hulick, one of the composers on the original trilogy of games, shared he was going to offer up his re-scored version of the trailer. Well, it's N7 Day, as rough as that will be for many of the developers on the game, but Hulick has stood by his word, and shared an oral glimpse at what the world of Mass Effect Universe could have sounded like.

You can check out this new version of the trailer below, perhaps pop it on while you have a read of the rest of this post, as Hulick did also write in a Patreon blog about his experience with the series. "As part of my own closure, and as a love letter to the fans, I really wanted to re-score the leaked Mass Effect Universe trailer in time for N7 Day this year," he wrote. "BioWare edited a killer track called “Quartz” by French 79 to fit their trailer, which I think hinted at a slight directional shift in the score. Using that as inspiration, along with the musical tone from my work on the first three Mass Effect games, I hit the studio and got to work."

As it turns out, BioWare had actually reached out to Hulick asking him to potentially do some work for the project. "This year, BioWare emailed me asking if I was interested in working with them on an internal demo, and they said they were 'hoping to get some music written that would feel like Mass [Effect], supporting our new direction.' Of course, I said yes!" This doesn't mean he wouldn't have composed the actual game, as a composer hadn't been officially selected, but Hulick said he was "more than happy to collaborate on the new game’s musical direction and help make their internal demo sound as good as it could."

Hulick also spoke of some of the ideas he had for the direction Mass Effect Universe's soundtrack could take, noting how he "thought it would’ve been incredible to bring more songs with lyrics into the game," considering how many people loved the first game's end-credits song. "This would’ve been a great way to highlight more emotional moments in the narrative. Artists at the top of my collaboration list included Gunship, NINA, and The Midnight, all of whom have a moody, cinematic synthwave vibe that would’ve meshed well with the score in my head. And I’m pretty sure the Gunship guys are gamers, and if they’re Mass Effect fans, that would’ve been the icing on the cake!"

Of course, with the studio now closing, this never got to go very far, the news hitting Hulick "particularly hard, as the Mass Effect trilogy is a core part of my origin story as a composer." For the composer, ultimately "this trailer is my way of saying goodbye… at least for now." This continues to be a horrendously sad moment in the industry! As always, solidarity with the team at BioWare.

Sicuramente è bravo a togliere linee di passaggio alla squadra avversaria. Tecnicamente è un giocatore completo. Stiamo lavorando tanto sull'evoluzione tecnica e sulla parte fisica. Tutti fanno riferimento a lui nello spogliatoio. Nelle ultime gare è stato importante, come tanti altri".

"As part of our ongoing quality testing of Pulse Elevate speakers, we have identified a production issue with the product and have made the decision to delay the launch," the statement reads. "The additional time will allow us to ensure the product will deliver the experience we intended for our players. We apologise for the delay and appreciate your patience and understanding. We look forward to bringing Pulse Elevate to players soon."

The speakers were set to go on sale worldwide on 12th November, and pre-orders opened in September. It's unclear what, exactly, happens to those orders, but we'll update as and when we find out more.

Sony revealed Pulse Elevate in September 2025 as its first wireless speakers for desktop gaming. They sit alongside the Pulse Elite headset and Pulse Explore earbuds, and cost £199.99 ($219.99).

They connect to PS5, PC, Mac, and PlayStation Portal via PlayStation Link, and can pair with a phone over Bluetooth at the same time. There's also a microphone in the right speaker with what Sony calls "AI-enhanced noise rejection".

Midnight Black was due to be available everywhere, while the White version was limited to a handful of countries, including the UK.

Alongside the Pulse Elevate speakers, Sony revealed a range of hardware in September 2025, including a 27-inch gaming monitor that's coming only to the US and Japan.

Palantir Stock Just Fell 8 Days in a Row. History Says What Followed Its Only 2 Longer Losing Streaks.

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Did the AI software company's two longer slides say anything about what happened next?

Palantir Technologies (PLTR +3.48%) stock closed lower for eight straight trading sessions through Thursday, Nov. 5. Over that span, shares dropped from $198.84 to $154.99, a fall of around 22%.

That put the AI software maker's stock about 26% under its record close on Oct. 9.

By my count of daily closes since Palantir started trading in September 2020, only two losing streaks have lasted longer. One was a 10-day slide ending May 10, 2021. The other was a nine-day slide ending March 1, 2023.

Each time, shares were up six months later. But a year out, the two cases ended up far apart. And two cases is a tiny sample, so I wouldn't rely on the streak to say much about what happens next.

Interestingly, both earlier slides were close in size to this one. The 2021 streak took the stock from $24.11 to $18.47, a fall of around 23%. The 2023 streak took shares from $10.11 to $7.69, a decline of around 24%.

(I measured what followed from each streak's final close, treating 21 trading days as a month.)

After the 2021 slide, Palantir stock gained around 31% in the next month and was up about 24% three months out. Six months later, it was about 41% above that final close. But a year after the slide ended, shares were down about 60% to $7.46.

The 2023 case went the other way over the full year. Shares were up only around 6% a month later. In the next three months, though, they rose by about 91%, and they were up 112% after six months.

Even more, one year out, the stock had more than tripled, gaining around 224% to $24.93.

The two seven-day streaks, which ended in September 2022 and June 2026, don't settle much either. After the 2022 slide, shares climbed about 16% in a month, dipped about 1% over three months, and were up about 12% after six months. A year out, the stock had more than doubled.

The June 2026 slide is too recent for a six-month read. But once it ended at $107.27, shares gained around 23% in the next month and about 80% in three.

So, after all four of Palantir's longest losing streaks before this one, the stock was up a month later. But with 12-month returns ranging from a 60% loss to a 224% gain, the streak arguably told investors little about the year ahead.

One difference between 2021 and 2023 sticks out to me, and it's the starting price. At $18.47, Palantir's stock traded at around 30 times its 2020 sales. At $7.69 in 2023, it cost under 9 times its 2022 sales. And Palantir lost money in both those years, though it had posted its first quarterly profit right before the 2023 slide started.

Today, Palantir's market value is more than 50 times its sales for the last four quarters, a higher sales multiple than in either case.

Sure, the business is very different now. Palantir's third-quarter revenue climbed 96% year over year to around $2.3 billion. This kept a streak of faster growth going, after rates of 70%, 85%, and 93% in the three quarters before that. And Palantir now turns a big part of its sales into profit, with a 47% operating margin last quarter.

Not every number is speeding up, though. The total contract value of the deals Palantir closed in the third quarter rose just 6% from a year before. The growth rate had already slowed sharply in the previous three quarters, dropping from 138% to 61% and then 49%. Deal timing can make the number lumpy. But if it keeps slowing, revenue growth might eventually follow.

Management's fourth-quarter revenue guidance of about $2.65 billion already implies growth of about 88%, down from the third quarter's 96%.

Palantir made $1.43 a share in its past four quarters. So at roughly $160 as I write, the stock trades at about 112 times earnings. Earlier this week, I put my buy price at 120 times those earnings, or about $172 a share.

In the end, the length of this streak doesn't tell me much about where Palantir stock heads next. What matters more to me is that shares now trade below my buy price.

Yes, a growth stock that just dropped 22% over eight sessions could easily keep swinging. But I think shares are worth a look at this level, and I'd build a position slowly instead of all at once.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Palantir Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Another 1-0 for relentless Arsenal - but did Odegaard spare VAR blushes?

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Martin Odegaard's goal gave Arsenal a fourth successive Premier League win

It was not pretty, but Martin Odegaard's goal was enough to secure the 1-0 win that ensured Arsenal go into the international break as the Premier League leaders.

It was Arsenal's 34th 1-0 win under Mikel Arteta, to equal their best start to a Premier League season under the Spaniard of 27 points earned after 10 games in 2022-23.

No team has enjoyed more 1-0 wins since Arteta was appointed back in 2019, with the Gunners winning their past 10 home games in all competitions, keeping eight clean sheets in that time.

Odegaard's goal though, bundled home from close range, may well have been a relief to those at Pro Ref, too.

A controversial video assistant referee (VAR) decision, which denied the Gunners a first-half penalty, threatened to be the story as a largely uneventful game drifted by.

When referee Farai Hallam was sent to the pitchside monitor for a VAR review for a shirt pull by Nobel Mendy on Kai Havertz, surely everyone inside the Emirates was expecting a penalty.

The irony would not be lost on Liverpool fans, who were denied a VAR penalty for a shirt pull last weekend when Arsenal's Jurrien Timber tugged back Alexander Isak.

Unlike Isak, Havertz made sure everyone - including VAR Michael Oliver, was aware that a defender had hold of his shirt.

Indeed, the cheers had already begun when Hallam started to describe the foul to the crowd - but Gunners supporters were stopped in their tracks. The referee pivoted to an offside against Havertz, and play restarted with a free-kick to Hull.

Had Arsenal not mustered that 72nd-minute winner from their captain, much of the attention would, once again, have been on VAR.

Tottenham set to complain to Pro Ref - but should Bogle have seen red?

Odegaard hits winner as Arsenal beat Hull to extend lead at top

Mikel Arteta post-match interview - Arsenal v Hull

If Arsenal had dropped points, it wouldn't have been the first time this weekend that a match would have been dominated by discussions about the video officials.

On Friday, Coventry City were denied what appeared to be a clear penalty against Everton for a hair pull by James Tarkowski on Caleb Yirenkyi. Then in the second half, when Thierno Barry scored to put the Toffees 2-1 up and the ball appeared to come off his hand.

The controversy continued in Saturday's early kick-off between Leeds and Tottenham. First, Leeds wanted a penalty for handball by Mateus Fernandes, though replays did show the ball hit the Spurs player's shoulder.

Then came the potential red card for a challenge by Jayden Bogle on Mathys Tel, which has split opinion. The VAR, Tony Harrington, eventually decided after a four-minute VAR check that the foul did not have enough force.

That Tel was forced off injured, and referee John Brooks did not even show a yellow card on the field, only added to the frustration for Tottenham.

So on to the Emirates, and again the debate was around VAR.

It's a situation few would question. After all, an offside player winning a penalty surely goes against the very logic of the law?

If an offside player is running to the ball and is fouled before they attempt to play the ball or challenge an opponent, then the foul is considered to come before the offside offence.

The law seeks to prevent situations whereby defenders have carte blanche to foul attackers, just because the opponent is offside.

But is a complex area of law and is very much open to interpretation.

In February 2019, the season before VAR came in, an offside Harry Kane was awarded a late penalty in a north London derby after being dragged down by Shkodran Mustafi.

Two years ago, Newcastle were awarded a spot-kick against Bournemouth through a VAR intervention when Adam Smith tugged back an offside Fabian Schar.

Yet last December, after being awarded a penalty against Leeds, Brentford's Dango Ouattara was ruled to be offside and the spot-kick ruled out through VAR.

So which interpretation is correct? Kane and Schar, or Havertz and Ouattara?

Ouattara was somewhat different, as he appeared to be engaging with Gabriel Gudmundsson as the ball was played.

The Havertz example is more like the one involving Schar, who was running forward when his shirt was tugged back.

But there is an additional consideration, and this is where Oliver's thinking will lie.

Officials are asked to consider the likelihood of the attacker becoming involved in the play from a second phase. Let's say the ball could be first played by a team-mate, which then makes them onside. Then they are not purely benefitting from being offside.

In the case of Schar and Kane, they could have benefitted from that second phase.

Havertz was the only attacker who had any chance of being involved in the play, so the VAR determined offside was the fair outcome.

At least tonight most of the talk will be about another victory for Arsenal - and not VAR.

Goal caps eye-catching Odegaard display - player ratings for Arsenal v Hull

Another game, another win but still Arsenal are looking for their attack to spark into life.

At the end of the day results matter but Arsenal, despite being top, are the lowest scorers of the teams in the top four.

They have the best defence in the league and arguably Europe but goals are also seeming hard to come by at the other end.

Arsenal have not scored more than twice in the league since the opening day of the season, when they put three past Coventry.

Arteta's side are missing talisman Bukayo Saka, who is out with a quadricep injury, and has a unique partnership with Ben White and Martin Odegaard on the right.

On the left Christos Tzolis started the season well but, after a hamstring injury while playing for Greece, the 24-year-old has not looked the same threat.

The same can be said for Kai Havertz too.

The 27-year-old picked up a hamstring injury playing for Germany and, even though he scored the winner against Liverpool, his performances have not reached the same level.

Viktor Gyokeres is firmly second choice this season and Arteta is still working out how to get the best out of the maverick Eberechi Eze, while Noni Madueke is working to fill the gap left by Saka.

Arteta was asked about the number of single-goal wins this season and what he can do to improve that.

"To be more efficient," was the answer the Gunners boss gave. "We are one against one against the keeper. That's happened twice today.

"There is a penalty there, there is another situation inside the box where the ball is bouncing. So many shots that have been deflected, blocked. We need to find ways to be more efficient, sometimes even more pragmatic.

"Sometimes my feeling is that we are trying to score a perfect goal against these low blocks. We need to be much more chaotic than that, and that's it. And open, I think, score much earlier because obviously that's going to change the course of the game."

Even if the Gunners could be more free-flowing going forward, they have started the season in a formidable way - winning nine of their 10 league games.

And if they manage to keep up that rate of winning, the Gunners will likely defend their Premier League crown and win back-to-back titles for the first time in 100 years.

Ginkgo Bioworks: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ginkgo's shift to physical data for AI labs is the thread tying its cost discipline to its hardware ambitions.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Ginkgo Bioworks (DNA +13.35%) announced a strategic transition toward commercializing autonomous laboratory hardware and cloud data services. For long-term investors, this operational evolution determines whether automated biology can build high-margin revenue streams while reducing cash burn. Three takeaways from management's call explain how this transition is unfolding.

Selling autonomous lab hardware to major biopharma partners validates Ginkgo Bioworks' technology while creating high customer switching costs. Pharmaceutical giant Novo Nordisk became the first to opt into this offering.

Cost cuts following restructuring efforts have improved adjusted EBITDA -- which is earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a key measure of core operating profitability -- while preserving cash.

Artificial intelligence (AI) developers increasingly rely on physical laboratory data generation to train complex reasoning and biological design AI models.

Demand for automated lab data accelerates as artificial intelligence developers seek physical experimental capacity. Modular architecture and cloud infrastructure provide scale advantages, while management monitors deployment efficiency.

Standardized hardware enclosures reduce setup times and support long-term recurring software revenue.

1983's Terrifying Sci-Fi Classic Officially Destroys the Competition on Prime Video

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Long before we started worrying about how artificial intelligence would take the photos of all of us dressed as if we were 1980s pop stars and use them to access our bank accounts, our social security numbers and our deepest, darkest secrets, Hollywood was already warning audiences about what might happen if computers were given a little too much responsibility.

From The Terminator to The Net, we've been lectured on how dangerous AI could be. Years before those two movies, we got an even more terrifying example of what could go wrong when presented with a scenario where a teenager could accidentally bring humanity to the brink of nuclear annihilation simply by trying to play a video game.

The 1983 sci-fi thriller WarGames has officially arrived on Prime Video in the United States, joining the platform on November 1. A teenager, bored by traditional high school subjects like biology, but fascinated by computers, accidentally taps into the Pentagon's top-secret computer...the USA's NORAD system. He starts what he innocently believes is a computer game called "Global Thermonuclear War," but the "game" is real.

The Pentagon's best minds cannot shut down or reprogram the supercomputer that is readying a missile attack against the USSR, and thereby unleash Armageddon. The teen and his girlfriend attempt to aid the helpless Pentagon and, in a frantic race against a ticking "doomsday clock," try to persuade the computer to end the "game."

The cast includes Matthew Broderick (Ferris Bueller's Day Off) as David Lightman, Ally Sheedy (The Breakfast Club) as Jennifer Mack, Dabney Coleman (9 to 5) as Dr. John McKittrick, John Wood (Chocolat) as Dr. Stephen Falken, Barry Corbin (Northern Exposure) as General Jack Beringer, Juanin Clay (The Edge of Night) as Pat Healy, Kent Williams (The West Wing) as Arthur Cabot, and Dennis Lipscomb (The Day After) as Lyle Watson.

The movie is directed by John Badham (Saturday Night Fever). Produced for approximately $12 million, WarGames earned $74.4 million during its original domestic theatrical run, according to The Numbers, while the film holds a 93% critics' score and 76% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes. Director Seth Gordon (Horrible Bosses) previously developed a modern WarGames remake for MGM centered around cyber-terrorism and the Stuxnet virus, but the project stalled and was eventually scrapped.

WarGames is streaming now on Prime Video in the United States. Stay tuned to Collider for all the latest news and updates.

You can easily keep your home network running during a power outage — here's how

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Stay online when the power goes out with the right backup hardware and a little planning.

If your power goes out, your laptop's battery will keep it up and running. It's a convenience that's not afforded to every electronic in your home. For example, networking gear, like a Wi-Fi mesh system or, more importantly, your router, will go dead silent in the event of an outage.

If you're on, say, an important video call for work, losing your connection could make for a rude interruption. It's even worse if you're mid-game, about to score that critical victory and secure bragging rights. Fortunately, there's an easy way to prevent interruptions in the event of a complete power outage.

The first step to keeping your network running in a power outage is a quick checkup on your networking equipment. If any of the devices between the wall jack and your laptop lose power, your connection goes with them. So, you'll want to understand that chain if you don't already. For cable internet, that means a modem and router, or a single gateway device that handles both.

If you have fiber internet, you'll have a router and an optical network terminal (ONT), or the box where the fiber line enters your home. Some providers install a battery backup on the ONT, although these are usually aimed at phone lines, not necessarily your internet connection. So check with your internet provider if you aren't sure.

Keep in mind that if your fiber ONT and router are in different rooms, you'll need two separate backup power units (or one with a long extension cord).

An uninterruptible power supply (UPS) is the best way to keep your internet up and running during a power outage. These devices sit between the power outlet and your networking gear, automatically switching to battery when your home's power goes out.

Like most products, they come in a range of prices. A modest one that can keep a router running for an hour or longer costs well under $100. More expensive models can last longer and even handle more power-hungry devices, like a PC.

It's worth doing a quick test run. Unplug the UPS from the wall when nobody at home is using the internet, and make sure everything stays online. If it does, you're ready for the next outage.

If you already own removable batteries for things like power tools and cordless vacuums, there's a cheaper option. You can buy small inverters compatible with your battery brand, whether it's DeWalt, Milwaukee or Ryobi.

There is a big downside. Unlike a UPS, inverters don't switch your networking gear over to battery power automatically, so you'll need to plug in the networking gear when the power goes out and wait for your equipment to restart. But on the positive side, inverters do let you swap batteries in and out if you get stuck in a longer outage.

As long as you supply the batteries, you can find inverters for as little as $30 for third-party models.

With a backup power source, it's easy to get carried away with keeping unessential devices working. However, whichever backup power option you use, plug in only your networking equipment; this isn't the place for your TV or game console.

And of course, this advice only applies to power outages where your internet provider's connection stays up. Your best option for a complete internet outage is your phone's mobile hotspot or a mobile hotspot device.

60 Minutes’ Lesley Stahl Addresses Backlash Over Her Decision To Stay Amid Bari Weiss’ Tenure

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Candiace Is One Of My Favorite Housewives And I’m Glad A RHOP Producer Stuck Up For Her After Andy Threw Shade

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Stahl is just one of a few holdovers after the firings.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

60 Minutes underwent a major creative overhaul this past summer, which saw executive producer Nick Bilton and CBS News Editor-in-Chief Bari Weiss make considerable staff changes. Several notable series alums were fired and, since then, several new employees have been added. However, when the show returned for Season 59 as part of the 2026 TV schedule, some familiar faces were still on the air, including host Lesley Stahl. Now, Stahl is getting candid about remaining with the show under Weiss’ leadership after the firings.

Stahl – who’s been with 60 Minutes since 1991 – opted to remain with the news magazine program alongside fellow series veterans Bill Whitaker and Jon Wertheim. Ahead of the trio jointly confirming their decision to stay, there had been considerable speculation about their plans. Stahl spoke about her choice while being honored at the Power 100 luncheon this past week and claimed that she’d been on the receiving end of backlash for staying with the show. Via The New York Post, Stahl explained why she and her colleagues chose to stay:

I myself have been under attack on social media because of my decision to stay at ’60 Minutes’ under the new regime. It was not an easy decision. It was one I made with my colleagues Bill Whitaker and John Wertheim. There was an urge to walk out and protest in solidarity with our colleagues who had been abruptly and cruelly fired. But we decided to stay out of a fear, a fear that if we didn’t, ’60 Minutes’ would vanish.

60 Minutes Alum On The Show’s Bari Weiss Era And One Thing She Still Finds ‘Disturbing’

On May 28, which has reportedly since been deemed “Black Thursday" within CBS News, 60 Minutes correspondents Cecilia Vega and Sharyn Alfonsi were fired alongside others like series EP Tanya Simon and executive editor Draggan Mihailovich. Days later, longtime correspondent Scott Pelley was fired as well after reportedly accusing Bari Weiss of “murdering” the show. In the months since then, Pelley, Vega and others have made claims of editorial interference against Weiss and claimed they were asked to exude bias in their reporting.

Bari Weiss herself has been at the center of backlash as well due to the claims of journalistic partiality. At this point, the Free Press founder has denied the assertions and emphasized her intention to improve 60. Since the show returned in September, ratings have reportedly been under the average of the previous season. Weiss addressed those concerns during a recent conference and stated that the series was doing “tremendously well.” Stahl also addressed that decrease in viewership while speaking at the luncheon:

Many loyal viewers over the last 58 years have tuned us out, we presume, in protest. To those who stopped watching, I’m asking you please to come back.

Back in June, Stahl discussed the state of 60 Minutes back in June. She addressed the wave of firings and referred to the fallout as the “hardest chapter” of her career. Stahl – who’s been in journalism for over 50 years – also said the series of firings “was by far the worst experience I’ve been involved in, or even witnessed.” At the time, Stahl also said she’d met with Bilton to stress that while she and her colleagues understood that more needed to be done to the show, she hoped that the broadcast wouldn’t be “tampered with.”

Since then, Bari Weiss and Nick Bilton have added Ross Douthat, Gianna Toboni, Trevor Phillips and Sebastian Junger in host and correspondent roles for 60 Minutes. Lesley Stahl’s future on the news program beyond this current season remains uncertain. In any case, it’s evident that a new era is currently taking shape not just at 60 but at CBS News as a whole.
