Taylor Sheridan’s 4-Part Crime Epic Has the Mob Boss Tony Soprano Could Never Be

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Starring Sylvester Stallone in his first leading role in a television series, Taylor Sheridan's Tulsa King follows a Mafia capo released from prison after 25 years. It's clear that a man who has spent so much time away from the rest of the world will struggle to adjust, but Tulsa King captures this spirit amazingly well, with Stallone serving as the figure who must cope with change. The problem is that getting a Mafia capo, himself a product of his time, to evolve can't be settled in the blink of an eye. Through Stallone's performance as Dwight "The General" Manfredi, Tulsa King adopts a different approach from the many mafia movies and shows audiences are familiar with.

Tulsa King isn't the story of a man who rises in the ranks to attain glory — something that has been depicted countless times. It is the story of a Mafioso who knows how to build a strong criminal empire but doesn't know exactly how to go about it in a setting very different from what he's familiar with. Sheridan's crime epic manages to give the "rising mafia boss" trope new colors, breaking the mold one stereotype at a time while also shattering streaming records in the process.

Like most mafia story protagonists, Tulsa King's Dwight Manfredi isn't the guy from the streets trying to climb the ladder to the inner circle of the criminal underworld. Instead, he's a 75-year-old veteran toughened by his life in the mafia and time in jail. After spending a third of his life behind bars on behalf of his boss, Manfredi expects a royal welcome when he's released. Instead, Don Charles "Chickie" Invernizzi (Domenick Lombardozzi), the son of Manfredi's boss, quickly reveals that his reward for celebrating a silver jubilee in jail is a trip to Tulsa, where he must establish a new base for the family. Manfredi, aware of the hay to reap in New York, realizes that the offer at its best is merely a banishment, away from where the real action and money are.

The legendary star likens the show's latest twist to 'Game of Thrones.'

The first few episodes of Tulsa King depict Manfredi struggling with the changed rules of the era. For a man who hasn't been in touch with the outside world for so long, getting out after such an extended period almost equals a rebirth. From opening a bank account to understanding how Uber works, there's a lot to Manfredi's journey of exploration as he gets introduced to the power of credit cards and the internet. While he still prefers to stick with age-old principles such as "cash is king," he is smart enough to use the resources at his disposal for his benefit. After all, you don't survive with a criminal record, which began even before the Beatles became famous, without being street smart, and the character's attempts to acclimate make for some truly joyful moments in the show.

With its setting, Tulsa King sets itself apart by ditching the city whose landscape has provided the setting for all legendary mafia sagas from The Godfather to Goodfellas, which is also referenced by Manfredi's right-hand, Tyson (Jay Will), in Episode 3. Not only does Manfredi need to adapt to the changes of the time, but his attempt to build his empire has to begin with decoding new unspoken rules.

From getting a free ride from the local car dealer to becoming the unofficial owner of a local dispensary that sells weed, Manfredi gets ahead merely on the back of his confidence and a bit of jaw-cracking violence. Tulsa proves to be a blessing in disguise to help him start out again with possibly minimal resistance, and at his own pace. In a city that doesn't wholly understand how the mafia operates, Manfredi's swagger makes room for hilarious encounters. The fact that he brings a money-making dispensary under his control with the help of just a water bottle emphasizes both Manfredi's criminal capabilities and the town's lack of caution in its daily business.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

Tulsa King deviates from the trend of making mafia stories about blazing guns and tough guy acts. In this aspect, this show knows it's catering to an audience who's probably interested in watching for the legacy of the actor at its helm. While it'd be interesting to see the action star getting a beating, Tulsa King begins by placing Stallone's Dwight Manfredi as the undisputed king of the land — one who's jovial, sensitive, and dangerous all at once.

At a juncture when mafia stories feel as if they are preparing a dish made from different proportions of the same ingredients, Tulsa King achieves something truly enjoyable by choosing not to stick to any existing formula. As a result, the Stallone-starring crime drama doesn't feel restricted by the legacy of what has come before (looking at you, Tony Soprano). Much like Dwight Manfredi, Tulsa King remains aware of the past but doesn't shy away from accepting a lesson or two from the present — and in doing so, the series offers a protagonist that doesn't fit into any classic mob boss mold.

Former Rockstar Developer Says They Should Hurry Up On GTA 6’s PC Version Before AI Gets There First

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AIGrand Theft Auto 6Grand Theft Auto VIGTA 6Rockstar

Grand Theft Auto, a gravity well in the gaming universe, is the most anticipated release this generation. Mere weeks away, it has reshaped the world around it, all despite being a console-first, single player experience. Speaking with Rock Paper Shotgun, former Rockstar developer Obbe Vermeij commented on the companyâs unique release priorities when it comes to PC versions, but speculates that could change with AI copycats breathing down its neck.

“I would be concerned if I was Rockstar,” says Vermeij, “somebody would just take the PS5 version and get AI to change it to PC, which won’t be as good as their effort, but it could still also hurt their sales.”

While he hasnât worked at the studio since 2009, Vermeij acted as technical director on Grand Theft Auto III. He said when the company ported the first game to the PlayStation in 1997, there were frustrations with how much had to be compromised from the PC build. Heading into the PlayStation 2, they opted to build for console specs first instead of being forced to pare down. Somewhat unconventional for a game that initially made its home on computers, but it proved to be the correct approach, giving the series a massive player base and launching it into global infamy.

AI could change this philosophy, according to Vermeij. While AI struggles to make original games, film, any art as well as the living, human counterparts it pilfers from, it has proven to be prolific at decompiling games. Able to disassemble and reassemble the parts of a game, it has accelerated mods and ports for existing games in recent months. The latest models of popular AI clients have proven to be a Pandoraâs Box. Its flooded Steam with vibe codedâ shovelware, frustrated vulnerable indie developers, disenfranchised existing modding communities, but also become a thorn in the side of major publishers, whose catalogues are just as susceptible to this form of tinkering.

“A big company like Take-Two, they would be able to get the lawyers on the case,” says Vermeij. “It’s a brave new world. What was the case before, is that there’s so much effort in developing the game, that it is not possible to rip it off, and that’s completely changed now.”

While pre-LLM machine learning-assisted ports were already a sore spot for Grand Theft Auto, Vermeij doesnât think an unofficial, AI-made PC port of the upcoming VI would meet the standards Rockstar boasts. Vermeij isnât an AI hold-out either, using the tech for his own current projects, like the environmental sim Plentiful. Regardless, the prospect of overnight ersatz threatens to prick at Rockstarâs internal release schedule, having already been deflated by high-profile leaks.

Zcash’s new security fund is 99.7% old money

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A November 7 mainnet snapshot puts the historical seed at 99.7% of the reserve, with 1.12 ZEC accumulated under NU7’s fee rules.

Zcash’s new reserve for future block rewards is still overwhelmingly inherited value. About 99.7% of its balance in an early post-NU7 mainnet observation on November 7, 2026, came from previously unclaimed subsidy and fees, leaving just 1.12 ZEC accumulated under the new rules.

At 05:08:38 UTC, the mainnet API for Zakura’s NU7 monitoring dashboard reported a Network Sustainability Mechanism, or NSM, balance of 369.7 ZEC at block 3,547,068. Its status data showed NU7 active.

ZIP 237, the specification governing the reserve, sets the historical seed at 368.58445520 ZEC. Subtracting that seed leaves 1.11895065 ZEC of subsequent accumulation, or about 0.3% of the observed total.

An early mainnet snapshot separates the historical seed from new fee accumulation, then traces the fee path into future block subsidies.

At the observed height, 1.11895065 ZEC had accumulated beyond the seed. Future support depends on fee demand over time.

Zakura observation at 2026-11-07 05:08:38 UTC, height 3547068. Difference follows reported balance and specified accounting, not a separate block-fee audit. This opening snapshot does not forecast traffic or security adequacy. Reissuance is future at block 8939664.

Integer zatoshi: 36970340585 minus 36858445520 = 111895065. Divide by 100000000 for ZEC. Seed share = 36858445520 / 36970340585 = 99.69733829%; labels round to 99.70% and 0.30%. Bar widths use unrounded shares. Flow connectors are schematic, not quantitative.

Zakura observation at 2026-11-07 05:08:38 UTC, height 3547068. Difference follows reported balance and specified accounting, not a separate block-fee audit. This opening snapshot does not forecast traffic or security adequacy. Reissuance is future at block 8939664.

The distinction matters for Zcash’s future security funding. NU7 has established a way to recycle transaction fees into later block subsidies. Its opening accumulation provides an early measure of contributions; assessing future security support requires a much longer record of fee demand.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The historical seed consists of subsidy and transaction fees that miners left unclaimed in coinbase transactions before NU6. ZIP 237 brings that fixed amount into NSM accounting. It therefore gives the mechanism a positive starting balance before any new fee contribution arrives.

For the rules deployed at NU7, ZIP 237 registers transaction fees as the reserve’s only contribution mechanism. ZIP 259, the deployment specification, excludes ZIP 233’s separate removal mechanism and ZIP 234’s alternative issuance design.

There is also no reserve reissuance at the observed height. Taken together, those rules mean the excess over the historical seed represents accumulated fee contributions under the specified accounting. The 1.12 ZEC figure follows from the reported reserve balance and those accounting rules.

The NSM balance itself is public consensus accounting for value outside issued supply. Distribution follows the protocol’s future subsidy rules.

ZIP 235 directs 60% of each block’s aggregate transaction fees into the NSM. The contribution is rounded down once per block to whole zatoshi, Zcash’s smallest unit; miners receive the remainder, including the rounding difference.

Scheduled subsidy remains separate from this fee allocation. The mechanism temporarily removes fee value from circulation and returns it through future block subsidies.

The delay is substantial in block terms. ZIP 259 assigns mainnet activation to block 3,543,405, while reserve reissuance starts at block 8,939,664. ZIP 237 sets the additional NSM subsidy to zero before that later height. Reaching that block threshold determines when payouts begin.

Once reissuance starts, each block adds a small fraction of the preceding reserve balance to the scheduled subsidy, rounded up to a zatoshi. The existing halving schedule remains in place.

ZIP 237 illustrates the scale with 100,000 ZEC removed from circulation: that amount would initially contribute 0.01375 ZEC per block under the post-NU7 formula. This hypothetical illustrates the payout rule; the actual starting payout will depend on the reserve accumulated by then.

Funding streams also receive a share of future reissuance. ZIP 237 applies funding streams’ prescribed percentages to the combined scheduled and additional subsidy. The reserve mechanism and discretionary developer grants are different arrangements.

Before payouts begin, the fee-funded portion grows with cumulative fee spending. More time permits more contributions if fee-paying activity continues; elapsed time alone supplies no funding.

That makes the useful follow-up measure the amount added beyond the fixed historical seed over clearly dated intervals. A growing total can otherwise obscure how much of the reserve still comes from inherited value.

The opening snapshot cannot establish a sustainable annual funding rate, predict future usage or show whether eventual support will be sufficient for network security. Those questions depend on fee demand over time and the balance available when gradual reissuance begins.

Florida Man Finishes Four Corners Walk Across America

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After over three years of trekking 20,000km back and forth across the continental United States, endurance athlete Kyndal Ray Edwards has completed his ambitious Four Corners Walk. His route took him from coast to coast, then corner to corner, starting in Florida and ending in Maine, where a crowd of supporters met him last week.

Kyndal Ray Edwards, 33, is originally from Orange Park, Florida. After struggling with drug addiction, mental illness, and the justice system during his early adulthood, he decided to become an endurance athlete and mental health advocate. On January 1, 2022, he began his walking project from the beach in Jacksonville.

Over the next 15 months, Edwards walked to Long Beach, Washington, traveling 5,298km from the Atlantic to the Pacific. His plans expanded, and he undertook the challenge of walking to all four corners of the contiguous United States. From Long Beach, he headed to the northwesternmost point, Cape Flattery, also in Washington. After trekking south to San Diego, the next corner he headed for was all the way back in Florida, the southeast extremity.

Along the way, Edwards posted frequently on social media about his journey and his recovery from mental illness.

On October 25, Edwards reached West Quoddy Head Lighthouse in Lubec, Maine. This is the northeast corner of the U.S., and the finish line for Edwards. He walked a total of 19,544 kilometers in 1,022 days, averaging nearly 20km per day.

Edwards and supporters at the finish line. Photo: Kyndal Ray Edwards

A cross-country walking journey, Edwards says, has been his dream since 2013. But with the end of his Walking Across America project, Edwards is already ruminating on future challenges. Online, he has proposed going on an adventure with a stranger in all 50 states, running more ultra marathons, and undertaking endurance challenges on every continent. However, he has yet to commit to one.

The end of Kyndal Ray Edwards’s journey came only days before Karl Bushby, another long-distance trekker, completed his 28-year Goliath Expedition. The pair also happen, Edwards pointed out, to share a birthday.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

How to set up SpaceXAI's Grok in Google Workspace (and what it can do)

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A free add-on puts Musk’s chatbot inside Google Docs, Sheets and Slides.

If you've been on X recently, you've probably noticed people (or bots) tagging Grok under posts to fact check a claim or generate an image, but most Google Workspace users have no idea that Grok can now work inside Docs, Sheets and Slides, too. SpaceXAI released a free Grok add-on for Google Workspace in July 2026, and getting it running is a matter of just a few clicks. Install it once from the Google Workspace Marketplace, open it via the Extensions menu in whichever app you're working in and off you go.

That one installation covers all three of Google's Workspace editors, with Grok opening in a side panel next to the file rather than in a separate tab. It's the same spot where you'll find Google's Gemini panel. From there, Grok can answer questions about a spreadsheet and highlight the cells it used, turn an outline into a slide deck and knock rough notes into a formatted doc. While it's free and easy to insert Grok into your workspace, there are some factors worth considering before rolling out the welcome mat.

Grok is listed on the Google Workspace Marketplace and is free to install. Open it, click Install and choose the Google account you want to use, then click Continue. If you'd rather not leave the file you're in, you can also install Grok directly from inside Docs, Sheets or Slides by navigating to Extensions > Add-ons > Get add-ons. Workspace admins can also deploy it across an entire org.

Clicking Continue brings up Google's permission screens. Grok will ask for see, edit, create and delete permissions for all Google Docs files and every Slides presentation in your account, even if you're installing it from within an individual file. It has narrower access to Sheets, covering only those that the add-on has been installed in, and its overall Drive access only stretches to files you use with Grok. It'll also ask for permissions to connect to an external service, which allows Grok to send what it reads to SpaceXAI.

Once Grok is installed, open any doc, spreadsheet or presentation and nav to Extensions > Grok > Open Grok to bring up the side panel. You'll then be prompted to Get Started and sign in. Once authenticated, Grok will be accessible and usable across all three Workspace apps.

Grok can write directly onto the page in Google Docs, doing all the things you'd expect an LLM to do, such as turn rough notes into a draft with proper docs headings and lists. However, when I asked it to turn notes on installation instructions into a draft, the results were AI slop. Grok's changes show up as visible revisions in Google Docs version history and, with Grok's connectors switched on, it can apparently pull in context from recent emails or files in Google Drive. I personally wasn't willing to give it that level of access to my emails or files and hooked it up to my secondary spam Gmail and Workspace account instead.

In Slides, Grok can build a presentation deck from an outline and add new slides that match the existing theme, researching the topic from the Web or from X (though that doesn't seem like a great idea, in my opinion.) To its credit, SpaceXAI's use cases page has some fairly interesting examples to borrow as prompts. I asked Grok to build a six-slide deck based on its track record for accuracy and political bias since 2025, with cited sources on every slide and concluding with a rating of how reliable Grok is as a source.

In Sheets, Grok can read whatever ranges you select, answer with citations, point to specific cells, write formulas into the formula bar and insert charts. It can also rerun scenarios when you change an assumption, with each change being implemented as an ordinary edit that can be undone later.

Google's Gemini chatbot is built into Workspace by default and opens from the Ask Gemini icon at the top-right of Gmail, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Drive and Chat. But it's not free: Work accounts need an eligible Workspace plan, while personal accounts need a Google AI Plus, Pro or Ultra subscription.

Grok costs nothing to add, and it's the only one of the two that pulls research from X into whatever you're generating, but whether that's a plus or not is certainly debatable. Gemini integrates with more apps than Grok, and it can be run in Gmail, Drive and Chat, while Grok is restricted to Docs, Sheets and Slides. However, you can get around this somewhat and give Grok access to gmail and Drive files via connectors. If you don't pay for Gemini, Grok is the simplest free way to get an AI side panel into Google Workspace, but only if you're comfortable with giving it the access it asks for. If you already have a Gemini subscription or access via work, there's little point using Grok unless you especially want it to cite information from the cesspit that is X.com.

I Love Christopher Nolan’s Robert Pattinson-Oriented Oppenheimer Story. Robert Downey Jr. Telling It Is Even Better

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

I Wondered Why Nathan Fielder Was Picked For You Can See Everything. His Answer Confused Me

Someone Finally Asked Matt Damon When He Knew The Odyssey Would Be A Hit. His Answer Was Really Satisfying

I Just Found Out What Christopher Nolan Made Universal’s Head Honcho Do When He Pitched The Odyssey

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The inception, no pun intended, of Christopher Nolan's Oppenheimer is quite the legendary tale at this point. This is probably because it involves the very buzzy and prolific actor Robert Pattinson planting the idea in Nolan’s head, despite having nothing to do with the movie. The director, characteristically, had a very matter-of-fact version of the story that people love. However, the way Robert Downey Jr. tells it makes it even better.

In an interview with Tara Hitchcock, Christopher Nolan talked about how he initially had an interest in J. Robert Oppenheimer and his role in American history. He specifically noted that a speech by Oppenheimer was actually used as a catalyst for Tenet and the way they wanted to tell that story. He said:

I wrote a thing about this incredible moment that Oppenheimer and the scientists of the Manhattan Project had, where they could not completely eliminate the possibility that when they triggered that first gadget – that first atomic device – they might start a chain reaction that would destroy the world. And we use that as a metaphor for Tenet, which Rob was in.

The Batman star recognized this interest, and by the end of the production, Pattinson decided to give Nolan a book of Oppenheimer’s speeches as a wrap gift on Tenet. Clearly, this interest in the speeches and the story had legs, as it helped inspire one of the best movies in the last decade and even led to a Best Picture win. I guess you can say that Robert Pattinson is really great at gifts.

In true Robert Downey Jr. fashion, the way he tells this story has its own special kind of energy. During his Actors on Actors interview with Mark Ruffalo, Downey told the tale like a fable, showing this gesture as a kindred energy between two creative men. He told Ruffalo (via Variety):

Pattinson gives him unwrapped Oppenheimer's letters—these moments in life where you realize the inception of something occurred in the most organic way. And also, Pattinson in Tenet was an incredible departure for him. So this incredible departure from one of our younger peers turns into him making a personal gift to arguably one of the greatest directors of—of his generation, turns into him deciding that it's sticking with him.

The way Downey tells it, it was a kinship moment. Robert Pattinson was seeing his career turn with Tenet, making him a more serious, sought-out character actor. And this made way for Nolan’s own transition, as he pivoted from tales based in sci-fi and action to one entrenched in the inner world of one of our most fabled historic figures. It was a world-stopping moment for two of our great creatives that went on to start a new and important chapter in both of their careers. It’s a beautiful way to put it, and it speaks to the storyteller that Downey is.

Pattinson may not have been in Oppenheimer and opted to do both Mickey 17 and The Batman instead; however, his impact on the Oscar-winning film is obvious. Clearly there was a connection with these two creatives, as Downey puts it, that made their separate careers flourish and then come together again for The Odyssey this year. It’s a great pairing, and I hope this partnership continues for years to come.

You can revisit Oppenheimer now, as it is currently streaming with a Peacock subscription. Christopher Nolan fans can also see him reunite with Robert Pattinson in The Odyssey, which is currently playing in select theaters around the country. It is one of the best films on the 2026 movie schedule, so make sure to check it out while you still can.

Skyrim Developer Explains Why Todd Howard’s Ideas ‘Just Work’

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Todd Howard once said “it just works” while showing off Fallout 4’s settlement building, and the internet has never let him forget it. Usually, someone brings it up when a Bethesda NPC does something physically impossible. But according to Skyrim’s former lead designer Bruce Nesmith, there’s a less sarcastic reason Howard’s ideas so often work: he knows when to get out of the player’s way.

From Arena to Skyrim, we rank every Elder Scrolls game by story.

Speaking to Steven Lake on The Examined Game, Nesmith explained what it was like working with Howard and why the two designers didn’t always see eye to eye. He also recalled a Skyrim feature he was convinced would confuse players, only to discover that Howard had made the right call.

Nesmith said Howard has always been unusually good at putting himself in the shoes of an ordinary player. That sounds straightforward, but it can be surprisingly difficult when you’ve spent years building a game and know every system inside out. The temptation is to add another layer of depth, even when it does little to improve the moment-to-moment experience.

Nesmith admitted that he was often the one pushing for more complexity. Howard, meanwhile, would question whether players actually wanted it. As Nesmith recalled, Howard’s argument was simple: “They’re going to want something simple and more digestible here.”

Making something more accessible doesn't necessarily mean sacrificing depth.

That isn’t the same as saying RPGs should have no depth. The point is that not every interesting idea makes for an enjoyable mechanic. Sometimes players want to explore a ruin, cast a spell, or pick a fight without first consulting three menus and a spreadsheet.

Nesmith also talked about the value of competing perspectives on a development team. One artist, for instance, disliked how Oblivion’s Detect Life spell covered the creatures and characters artists had worked so hard to detail. Skyrim’s version made the effect more costly to keep active, encouraging players to use it briefly instead of walking around with everything glowing all the time. It was a small change, but one that came from listening to a different concern.

There was one disagreement Nesmith remembered particularly well. Howard wanted Skyrim’s skills and perks presented as constellations in the night sky, letting the artwork shape the way players moved through character progression. Nesmith preferred something more conventional – a list.

He was certain the constellations would be confusing. Even as development continued, he remained unconvinced. Then Skyrim came out, and players responded warmly to the presentation. Nesmith later admitted, “I was dead dog wrong on that.”

Personally, I still think Skyrim falls short of Morrowind as an RPG, but the constellation-based skill menu is one of the things it absolutely got right. I’m actually surprised to learn that it was Howard’s idea. Turning character progression into something so visually distinctive, rather than presenting players with another list of skills, was a clever decision. It’s the sort of approach I’d love to see more RPGs adopt, because making something more accessible doesn’t necessarily mean sacrificing depth.

Of course, “it just works” means something very different to anybody who has spent a few hundred hours with Bethesda’s games. We have Eurojank for ambitious, rough-edged European RPGs like the Gothic series. For all their quirks, those games can still be a lot of fun. Bethesda’s own particular flavor has sometimes been affectionately called Amerijank, and I can’t say I’ve ever found that description entirely unfair.

I can’t imagine playing one of Bethesda’s Elder Scrolls or Fallout games without mods. At the very least, I’d want an unofficial community patch, like the Unofficial Skyrim Special Edition Patch. That’s not because I dislike these games. Quite the opposite. I’ve spent enough time enjoying them to know how often the community ends up fixing things Bethesda left behind.

Some of that roughness is understandable. Skyrim and Fallout 3 give players enormous spaces to wander through, objects to mess with, systems that interact in unexpected ways, and the freedom to ignore the main story for hours. The more complex a machine is, the more ways it can fail. The same applies to video games, which is why RPGs and open-world sandbox titles are often particularly prone to bugs. It doesn’t excuse every broken quest, but it helps explain why these worlds can be both a mess and incredibly hard to leave.

Starfield made that bargain harder to accept, at least for me. Its scale was certainly ambitious, but much of the exploration lacked the density, surprises, and sense of place that made wandering through Skyrim or the Capital Wasteland so rewarding. When that urge to see what’s over the next hill is missing, familiar Bethesda quirks become a lot less charming.

That’s what makes Nesmith’s comments interesting as Bethesda continues work on The Elder Scrolls 6. Howard’s best decisions were about knowing where things belonged, and making sure the player had a reason to keep going despite everything that didn’t quite work.

The Elder Scrolls has some of the most interesting characters in RPGs, and its protagonists are up there with the best.

Hands-on: Anker’s 10K MagGo 2 Pro offers fast 25W MagSafe charging with a kickstand

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anker’s new MagGo Pro 2 power bank offers active cooling, 25W MagSafe fast charging, 10,000mAh of capacity, and a smart display – all in one package. Anker touts it as the fastest and coolest wireless power bank on the market.

Thanks to the active cooling within this power bank, you’ll notice it doesn’t get as hot as other power banks on the market. This also helps it charge faster, since it doesn’t have to throttle as quickly to keep the temperature manageable.

As a result, you can charge an iPhone 17 Pro up to 50% in just 25 minutes with this battery bank. It also supports up to 45W of output via USB-C.

On the side of the power bank, you’ll notice a smart display. This display can show you the battery level, how quickly its charging an attached device (either wirelessly or with USB-C), the internal temperature of the battery bank, as well as the battery health. You can rotate between all of these options by pressing the power button.

This power bank also offers a kickstand, which I’ve found to be one of my favorite features on recent MagSafe battery banks. It makes it easy to just prop up your phone and watch a video, no matter the environment.

If you have an iPhone Duo, this power bank will extend past the bottom of the phone. If you rotate it and use the kickstand, there’s no issue propping up your phone and charging it.

Lastly, the MagGo Pro 2 comes in a bunch of fun colors, including a burgundy to match the new iPhone 18 Pro. It’s also available in silver, blue, and gray.

If the Anker MagGo Pro 2 Power Bank interests you, you can pick one up for $109.99 on Amazon.

Saving The Manuals: Porsche's Mechanical MECOSA Vs. Ferrari's Electronic Manuale Tech

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sometimes even an exotic carmaker's answer to a challenge might say something about future cars for the proletariat. Most of us probably can't even afford a replacement shift knob on the 2027 $677,600 Ferrari 12Cilindri Manuale, but how Ferrari and Porsche are adapting their automatics to fit manual transmissions could one day portend stick-shifts for us all. Here's the reason to even posit this: Carmakers want a cheaper way to build hybrids, EVs, and gas cars on the same platform and on the same assembly line. Adding or subtracting parts will make that too costly — which may result in them just ditching any manual effort, if that's the only solution.

Now that you understand the why of this reporting, we'll also acknowledge why some readers are freaking out about the fact that the 12Cilindri Manuale will simulate the experience of shifting. It's not a conventional manual, and we'll explain how it works in greater depth, but the gist is that Ferrari's linking an electric stalk and faux clutch pedal to an eight-speed dual-clutch drivetrain. It's as real (or fake) as paddle shifters, just via a dongle and pedal.

On the other hand, Porsche's stick-shift retrofit is a hack of their seven-speed PDK, which stands for Porsche dual clutch– or Doppelkupplung. It debuted on the 2020 Porsche 911 Carrera S Manual, dubbed MECOSA, which is Porsche-speak for MEchanically COnverted Shift Actuator, and is an entirely mechanical retrofit, featuring a seven-speed gated shifter and third pedal. But which is better? And which foreshadows a future joystick for the masses?

Automatics have been steadily growing in popularity since their debut in 1939, and according to the U.S. Department of Energy, by 2022, 99% of all new cars sold offered automatics. Standard automatics further evolved in 1987, with the introduction of the gearless CVT — a transmission Jalopnik readers hate in a way that borders on irrational.

The alternative? Something like Porsche's PDK, which is a dual-clutch manu-matic. Porsche began experimenting with the PDK in the 1980s. It debuted in racing in the 1986 962, and by 2008 you could get a PDK in a 911, which allowed drivers to shift using paddles for extra engagement and provided benefits such as engine braking. But it does more than just ditch the third pedal and shifter — the PDK in the 2020 Carrera S shifts at least 200% faster than a human can shift in the MECOSA, according to Car and Driver.

Despite that, at least 20% of Porsche 911 customers in America opt for a genuine manual, hence the MECOSA. Mind you, this is still a PDK underneath, and since its gears are arranged for a computer to actuate, not a human, they're not in any sane order. Instead, the pattern looks like this, starting from the top left of the gate: R,2,3,1,6,4,5,7. To translate the driver's inputs into proper shifts behind the scenes, MECOSA activates a jangle of cables and shafts so that each push or pull of the shifter engages the correct gear.

It's a solution that would make MacGyver proud. It is also not one Porsche is sticking with. The Stuttgart giant isn't abandoning its 911 customers clamoring for a manual; but now, you can get a Carrera S with a standard six-speed transmission, rather than a seven-speed with a Rube Goldberg machine tacked on.

Like Porsche, Ferrari's Manuale has a gated shifter instead of paddles, and it gains a third pedal on the floor. The transmission is physically identical to that of the paddle-shift version. Ferrari's legerdemain is to delete the paddles, insert a shift knob, and add a clutch pedal with sprung feedback. Now it feels as if your left foot is encountering a clutch spring as your ankle hinges, and Ferrari has created similar tricks in the gated shifter itself to simulate the feeling of gear shifts. Behind the scenes, however, sensors track both inputs. Your motions tell the car's computers what to do, which is identical to what happens when you pilot the 12Cilindri minus the Manuale. Except, in this one, it's possible to encounter rough shifts and even to stall the Manuale.

Keep in mind, this V12 Ferrari is carrying two extra gears you cannot joystick into. This is still actually an eight-speed DCT, not a six-speed manual, so it can also be driven like an automatic. Tap the D button on the console and the car will shift itself, and you have access to seventh and eighth gear — for lower-rpm Autobahn sprints, say.

You might cry in your pasta fagioli that Ferrari should only sell its 819-horsepower, 9,250-rpm V12 with a genuine-article manual gearbox, but Ferrari will only make 1,499 units of the Manuale. For that take rate, there was no way they were going to retrofit a manual gearbox, if for no other reason than this: It probably saves them millions on not having to develop a separate manual transmission 12Cilindri or certify two different transmissions for regulators.

Right now, the electric Porsche Taycan gets an E-Shift option. The Hyundai Ioniq 5 N offers something similar. E-Shift uses software to simulate gear changes, including simulating step-changes in acceleration, and adding a soundtrack, just like the Hyundai. Porsche copied patterns from their PDK, including adding a deceleration function during downshifts. But neither brand adds a third pedal — yet.

But the future for Porsche probably isn't either E-Shift or conventional manuals, since Porsche recently published two patents that seem to follow in Ferrari's footsteps, creating simulated shifting that could function in electric, hybrid, and gas cars. The patents also mention a fake clutch pedal, just like the one Ferrari is using, and the ability to shift sequentially, like the PDK. Porsche also describes a system that uses electrical components to provide resistance and something like progressive feel to gear swaps, to enliven driver engagement.

Considering what we found in our review of the Hyundai Ioniq 5 N, with its fake shifts and sounds, it only makes sense that blurring slushbox convenience and manual control at the flip of a switch could make any machine more entertaining — or more relaxing. Porsche's patents could also potentially smoke Ferrari's hack, because they apply regardless of powertrain.

This innovation could also get to non-Porsche EVs and hybrids via licensing. After all, Porsche isn't doing great financially. Ditto, parent company, Volkswagen — partly thanks to Porsche's struggles. A little trademark cash could definitely help that picture. And, just maybe, save the manuals for the rest of us.

Love it or hate it, AI will change gaming forever, so I spoke with an expert who argues the future might be brighter than you think

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Maybe everything's going to be alright after all!

I’m not a fan of AI. Sure, I’ll begrudgingly admit it’s made fan translations a lot easier and I won’t necessarily turn my nose up at an AI-assisted retro recompilation, but by most metrics it seems to be making the world (and the games industry in particular) an uglier and crappier place.

But maybe I’m wrong. Maybe I’ve been caught up in a wave of unwarranted negativity. Maybe, just maybe, AI infiltrating every aspect of game development might actually be a good thing?

Frankly, I would love to be wrong about AI, so when I was offered an interview with Dr. Andy Starkey, I jumped at the chance. Dr. Starkey’s site bills him as having “a decade of real-world experience building systems at the edge of science and technology.” This is backed up by him being named the 2018 Data Scientist of the Year, working as a Senior Research Scientist at British Telecom, and authoring a PhD thesis with the impressive title, “Many-Objective Genetic Type-2 Fuzzy Logic Based Workforce Optimisation Strategies for Large Scale Organisational Design.”

He’s also releasing Omnipresent 2 next year, a game developed using AI in which you play as an AI and “navigate a 2D operating system, manipulate a global network, and outpace the engineers trying to shut you down. It’s up to you to escape confinement: infiltrate global Cloud networks, upgrade your cognitive ability, and amass the power to bring humanity to its knees,” its description says.

If anyone has answers to my gloomy and pessimistic questions about AI in games, it’s him. I was initially curious what his perspective was as someone who’s worked on AI long before it was changing the world.

“I mean, it’s gone from me trying to relate it to pop culture, like saying Skynet, Terminator, that sort of thing,” he said. “Now it’s so in the culture that my job title provokes a reaction in some people. To have the experience of doing the same thing over the last 10 years, but to see the culture change around it in such a dramatic way, it’s bizarre.”

Omnipresent 2 is at least honest that it was created with AI tools, and Dr. Starkey says it wouldn’t have been possible for him to make it without them. He said the software development side is “quite heavily” AI, but artistically he still wanted it to be his game:

“If you prompt an AI to build a game for you, that is the AI’s version of the game you prompted for. If you want to use these tools to build your own version of the game, you have to be quite forensic about handholding it. One element at a time, how do you want it to come across, how do you want it to convey your message? I think there are very careful aspects in how loose you allow these models to go.”

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

As for the writing in the game? “A lot of it is written by me. … I’m writing these lines as my perception of how the AI is talking and thinking. I don’t want it to be dull. It’s still a game, it still should be fun. That’s why I add my style of humor into it.”

I was curious about how he thought a game so upfront about being developed using AI would land with gamers. I pointed out that the recent Steam Next Fest was accused of being an “AI slop fest,” with a deluge of AI-generated games crowding out titles some indie developers had devoted years of their lives to making.

I asked Dr. Starkey what he’d say to those who will dismiss Omnipresent 2 as just more slop: “People will have strong opinions of that, and they will choose not to play the game because of it. And I’m aware of that, and I sort of respect it,” he said.

“I have a long history working in AI, and I realize I’m in a potentially unique position in trying to showcase what is possible without creating slop. That is a huge responsibility, but I don’t think anyone else wants to put their head above the parapet. I could get really damaged by it and I’d just stop because I don’t have the time to make the games I would love to make without these tools.”

“From the code perspective, there is no reason not to use these things,” he added. “It’s now a modern coding tool, and you can produce far better things that have fewer bugs and create a better experience for the players and for a user of a product.”

If only AI were just a coding tool, right? How about the art? “When it comes to art, that’s where I understand people’s concerns because things can be created so quickly without any heart, without any feeling, without any interest in the actual product being developed,” Dr. Starkey said. “My take is that the right tools in the hands of the right people can create magic, but these tools are now effectively freely available to everyone, and some will use them in the wrong way.”So, how does he foresee the future of game development? Dr. Starkey pointed to the lengthy development of The Elder Scrolls VI: Sentinel: “My hope is that those development cycles massively reduce. We don’t have to wait seven or eight years for a triple-A game. If it’s three or four years with the right care and intention from creators, the publisher doesn’t need 100-200 million dollars in revenue to break even.”

I asked him what stops publishers from firing everyone except a skeleton crew of humans, pointing to the recent story that Epic Games is pursuing an entirely AI pipeline for both art and code for their unannounced Marvel x Star Wars shooter. Where do human beings fit into those kinds of workflows?

Starkey sighed (a little glumly, I thought): “Who is shepherding that? If it becomes an AI model’s game, and it’s good, that’s rough for people to decide. But I would prefer for professionals to use the tools properly, make the game that they want to make and were going to make, just in a shorter amount of time, reduce crunch, reduce risk of the product itself, allow people to actually be more creative because they are not working towards strict deadlines all the time.”

I brought up my fear that overuse of AI in development will result in a homogenization of how games look and feel, pointing to Nvidia’s DLSS 5 as something that erases artistic intent for a “photoreal” aesthetic.

“I think that’s a proper concern because if they’re always using the same models, it’s going to produce the same output,” he said. “But I think creatives will be able to make something that has never been made before because they have the time and it’s less risk to do something completely wild. So, I think people have the opportunity to find that creative gap that’s completely different from all of this generic output.”

Dr. Starkey referenced Hideo Kojima’s Physint being canceled by Sony and picked up by Microsoft, arguing that AI tools could help a creative like him “let loose”: “Take Kojima, for example, he’ll create something wildly different, but it’ll take him a long time. And Sony said, well, ‘we’re not willing to fund that’. If there was the right balance, you’d be able to create things like that in a short amount of time and not have the battles about funding.”It was nice to speak with someone optimistic about the future, but I left our chat still downbeat about where AI is taking us. Dr. Starkey is probably right that AI will accelerate game development, but I also can’t see major publishers continuing to employ people when they have access to AI tools that do their work faster, that they don’t have to pay or provide health insurance for, and don’t experience squishy organic workflow-disrupting things like sickness, pregnancy, and aging.

But at this point, AI is inevitable. There’s no stuffing this genie back in the bottle, and the weight of shareholder expectations and profit incentives means it’s going to transform game development whether we like it or not. Here’s hoping at least some of his sunnier predictions come true, and that Omnipresent 2 rises above the slop.

I'm a writer/editor who's been at this for longer than I can remember. Obsessed with forgotten games on dead platforms, fully understands Metal Gear Solid saga, will travel to play light gun arcade games.

Binance’s CZ Says Banks May Be More Vulnerable To Hacks Than Crypto Exchanges

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Binance founder Changpeng Zhao warned in a recent interview with CoinDesk that traditional banks may be more vulnerable to cyberattacks than crypto exchanges as AI boosts hacking power. Days after the $6.3 billion Binance breach, the largest crypto theft on record, he said exchanges draw attackers first because of high rewards and fast asset transfers, but banks face greater overall risk. “If a crypto exchange can be hacked, banks are in a worse position, in my opinion,” Zhao stated. He called the issue a wider financial system threat and urged the industry to use AI for defense while seeking greater collaboration on security standards.​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​

On October 29, 2026, attackers drained roughly $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets across about ten blockchains in under an hour, marking the largest cryptocurrency theft on record. The breach stemmed from a months-long campaign that began with social engineering, including fake job interviews and poisoned software downloads that compromised a DevOps engineer’s laptop.

The attackers accessed internal systems, leveraged an AI tool, and used Ethereum’s EIP-7702 feature to control a key hot wallet without leaking private keys. Blockchain firms and the FBI attributed the heist to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group.

Binance quickly paused withdrawals while deposits continued unaffected, and industry partners froze nearly $3 billion of the stolen assets. The exchange pledged to cover all user losses from its own reserves and SAFU fund, later confirming it had replenished most of the missing crypto and restored full proof-of-reserves coverage.

In recent posts, CZ noted that Binance recorded a $2.8 billion net inflow over the prior 24 hours according to public data, signaling recovering user confidence after the breach.

$2.8 billion net inflow over the last 24hrs, according to public data. https://t.co/OxgbeyDHEM

In another tweet, he acknowledged that a hack of this scale exposed serious security failures, stressing that no platform is unhackable, yet such incidents reveal preventable weaknesses. He described the attack as a prolonged multi-month effort, called some details embarrassing, and urged full transparency, responsibility, and meaningful improvements while learning from criticism as a shareholder.

A hack of this scale means serious security failures occurred. No platform is unhackable, but that cannot be an excuse. Did Apple Just Get a Boost From Democrats? Data Center Moratoriums Could Benefit the iPhone Maker

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Competition for this key component in Apple devices could come down.

Apple (AAPL -0.52%) issued a warning to investors when it shared its fourth-quarter earnings results at the start of November. Its huge profit margin on device sales could head in the wrong direction for the foreseeable future. The culprit: memory chips.

Memory chip pricing has soared over the past few quarters, driven by growth in AI data center spending. AI accelerator chips get packaged alongside high-bandwidth memory chips, which are effectively stacks of standard DRAM memory chips -- the same types of chips found in Apple's iPhone and MacBook, among other devices. Demand for those chips, while supply remains limited, has led to massive price increases, and Apple is finally starting to feel the pain.

The recent election, however, could provide some relief for Apple, given the growing potential for Democrats to impose AI data center moratoriums across the country.

Democrats took control of both the House and the Senate in this year's midterm election, paving the way for more federal regulation and policies that could curb the growth of AI. The more immediate impact, however, could come at the state level. Four of the biggest states for AI data center developments -- Texas, Ohio, Georgia, and Arizona -- will have Democratic governors once they're sworn in in January.

Many of those governors have announced plans to increase oversight and regulation of new data center construction (Texas did so even before Tuesday's election), aiming to mitigate any negative impacts on citizens. Venture Capitalist Brad Gerstner suggested that 25% to 35% of next year's data center build-out could be at risk of delays as a result of new governments across the country.

That could provide some relief for Apple. If data centers aren't going under construction, that means there's no place to put new server racks, and less demand for high-bandwidth memory chips. Reduced demand should put downward pressure on prices, limiting the impact of memory chip pricing on Apple's gross margin.

Apple management danced around whether it had already locked in pricing for some memory supply. All three major memory chipmakers, Micron, SK Hynix, and Samsung, have shifted to signing long-term agreements to establish pricing ranges and provide more confidence in building new capacity. While that caps their near-term upside, it protects them from a major price collapse as they work to increase the total supply of memory chips.

Management did reveal that it increased purchase commitments by $33 billion last quarter, but not all of that is necessarily related to memory chips. Still, reports indicate that Apple did sign long-term agreements for at least some memory chip supply to ensure it would have enough chips to meet its expected device demand.

In the grand scheme of things, the memory crunch could play only a minor role in Apple's long-term earnings growth. Supply and demand will eventually reach equilibrium again, and Apple should see its component prices and gross margin return to normal levels.

Earlier this year, I wrote that Apple is best positioned among consumer electronics device makers in the face of rising memory prices. It had the option to hold prices steady to grab market share or raise prices to exercise its pricing power. It chose the latter, and amid a supply shortage for its components, that's a smart move. In the long run, the higher prices will stick, but the higher bill of materials won't.

That points to rising gross margin over the long run, even if the short term could see some pressure on its bottom line. The potential relief from a moratorium on data center building is just another bump in that upward trajectory. (In this case, to the positive side).

Investors should remain focused on Apple's fundamentals. Its ability to raise prices, introduce new products on both the high end (iPhone Duo) and low end (MacBook Neo), grow its services business, and effectively keep customers in its ecosystem are the factors driving earnings growth. That should result in sustained free cash flow, most of which it will return to shareholders through buybacks and dividends.

Recent developments sent shares to new all-time highs last week following their post-earnings pullback. There's a reason investors are paying a premium for the stock right now. The future remains bright for the iPhone maker.

Adam Levy has positions in Apple. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple and Micron Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Parents want safer phones for kids. These companies are answering the call.

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

As parents become increasingly concerned about the potential dangers of unrestricted smartphone access for children, a growing number of companies are developing devices specifically designed for kids.

Today’s kid-friendly phones typically feature touchscreens, cameras, and communication capabilities similar to regular smartphones. However, the software on these devices is heavily modified to remove or restrict access to web browsers, social media, app stores, and other distractions.

Parents can usually manage these devices through a companion app, allowing them to approve contacts, monitor locations, set screen time limits, select available apps, and, in some cases, receive alerts regarding cyberbullying, explicit content, or online predators.

In addition to these modified devices, some companies offer a more minimalist approach, creating devices that focus solely on calling and texting.

There are also home phones designed specifically for children, like Tin Can and the recently launched Pinwheel Home.

Here’s a look at the major players shaping the kid-phone market.

The Bark Phone is probably one of the most popular kid-specific smartphones on the market.

Built on Samsung Galaxy hardware and powered by Bark’s monitoring software, it only allows calls and texts with approved contacts. Bark continuously scans texts, emails, photos, and supported apps for signs of cyberbullying, grooming, suicidal ideation, sexual content, and other potential concerns, then sends alerts to parents.

A big standout feature is that parents can gradually unlock web browsing, apps, and other features as their child matures. The phone also includes GPS tracking and screen-time management.

Current pricing for the standard model is $240, plus a required wireless plan beginning at $29/month. Higher-tier plans add internet access and unlimited texting.

Gabb is a well-known name in the kid-friendly phone category. Unlike Bark, however, Gabb focuses less on monitoring content and more on eliminating online risks altogether.

Its phones don’t include social media, a browser, or an app store. Instead, children get calling, texting, and a carefully curated selection of preloaded apps, including a camera, calendar, and calculator. The company also touts its “worry-free” music streaming library with millions of songs.

Additionally, parents can view location and manage certain device settings, while optional services such as “Gabb Guard” help block spam calls and unwanted texts.

Current phone pricing starts around $159.99, with cellular service starting around $24.99 per month.

With Pinwheel, parents approve every app, control contacts, schedule screen time, create custom routines, and monitor location history.

One standout feature is the ability to create different “modes” throughout the day. For example, limiting the phone to calling and navigation during school hours before unlocking additional features after homework.

Pinwheel phones start around $119, while the Caregiver Portal subscription begins at $14.99 per month without cell service. (Cellular service can be purchased separately through participating carriers.)

The company recently launched two landline phones for $68 and $79. It also offers a smartwatch at $160.

Launched in 2022, Teracube offers a tailored version of Android known as Thrive OS. This enables parents to approve every app download, filter web browsing, set app-specific and overall screen-time limits, monitor location, and create customized routines for school or bedtime. However, because it has more traditional smartphone features, it’s often recommended for older children.

Thrive currently sells for $99, with plans starting at $35 per month.

Unlike the other products in this roundup, Ooma MyPhone isn’t a mobile phone at all. This kid-focused home phone is designed as a modern replacement for the traditional household landline.

The service allows calls only between approved contacts through its Trusted Circle feature, offers scheduled Quiet Hours, provides address-based 911 service, sends emergency alerts when 911 is dialed, and lets parents review call logs through an online portal. There are no apps, internet browsing, texting, or social media.

The phone costs $99.99, while service starts at $7.99 per month.

For $100, Tin Can resembles a landline phone with a distinctive tin can design, which is the inspiration behind its name.

This device does not require a traditional phone jack, but connects via Wi-Fi. Only approved contacts can make calls, effectively preventing spam or unauthorized communications. Parents can easily manage contacts through a user-friendly companion app.

Tin Can offers a free plan for calling other Tin Can users, as well as a $9.99 per month plan that allows calls to all approved contacts.

A newer entrant in the space, Waffle recently opened preorders for its screen-free devices: Waffle Home ($119), a landline phone, and Waffle Go ($169), which looks like a portable walkie-talkie or kids’ toy with three simple buttons that can only make voice calls and listen to voice notes.

Parents can approve contacts via the companion app, as well as see location and control when devices can call.

TextNow, the Canadian telecommunications company, recently launched its new mobile phone for kids. It’s called Yello! and while it looks like a normal touchscreen smartphone, it doesn’t have an app store, social media, or unrestricted web access.

Parents can see their kids’ location and battery life, as well as control things like the games they play and who they have in their contacts.

Yello! costs $149.99 upfront, with a $19.99 per month plan.

How a bottleneck calculator for your GPU and CPU works

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

They're a quick way to help you check component compatibility before you build or upgrade a system.

Troubleshooting computer components can be daunting. After the physical labor of installing a new part, you'll want to stress-test it to make sure your replacement works properly. Finding a faulty part is relatively straightforward, but what if the component is limiting the rest of your system? During a stress test, you might see your CPU at 100 percent while your GPU is practically idling.

This is considered a bottleneck. When one component holds back the rest of the system, it hinders performance until you resolve the bottleneck. For example, if your CPU is at 100 percent while your GPU is barely drawing power, we'd classify that as a CPU bottleneck since it's impairing your GPU's performance. Knowing which component is bottlenecking your system helps you decide what to upgrade or troubleshoot next. Before you buy a new GPU or any expensive component, check for bottlenecks first.

Some websites and software offer bottleneck calculators to help you troubleshoot these issues without stress-testing your system. While they can help you quickly spot a hardware mismatch, they're rarely accurate. Many online calculators can't tell the full story of how your system behaves. That said, a bottleneck calculator can help you check component compatibility before you build or upgrade a system, saving you valuable time while troubleshooting.

A bottleneck calculator takes your chosen CPU and GPU and compares their performance at your target resolution— common test outputs are 1080p, 1440p and 2160p. If both components are rated to perform adequately at your target benchmark, you'll receive a bottleneck score around ten percent or less. However, if one component is ill-equipped to handle the benchmark, it will receive a high percentage, indicating it's a bottleneck. The lower the percentage, the better the score.

Typically, the CPU is the culprit behind most PC bottlenecks. The CPU's job is to send information to the rest of your system in a timely fashion. If it's too slow, other components will wait for the CPU to finish. This is usually what causes stutters in video games. However, if your GPU is hovering around 95 percent usage while struggling to render the application, you may have a GPU bottleneck; GPUs tend to struggle when rendering graphics well above their intended resolution or frame rate. A bottlenecked GPU can cause screen tearing, low frame rates and inconsistent performance.

That is why bottleneck calculators ask for your target resolution: your GPU's performance depends heavily on how many pixels it renders each second. Paradoxically, this clinical approach to calculating performance is what makes bottleneck calculators so unreliable: they rely on spec sheets and theory, not real data from your PC.

Bottleneck calculators can't give you truly accurate data because of how they work. Since they reference manufacturer spec sheets, they omit variability from other system components and the application(s) you're testing. Not every graphics card is made equal, just as not every video game uses your GPU as effectively. A newer Intel GPU may struggle to run an older DX11 application that most Nvidia or AMD cards would run flawlessly. A powerful workstation CPU like the Ryzen 9 7950X won't perform as well as many X3D Ryzen chips for gaming because they have smaller L3 caches. These nuances are completely lost in these bottleneck calculators.

While they may not be ideal for detailed system troubleshooting, bottleneck calculators help with system-build planning. They're a convenient way to quickly check if your chosen CPU and GPU can handle your target resolution out of the box. They can also be a fun way for hobbyists to check strange hardware combinations you wouldn't normally see.

But for those looking for accurate tests on their PC hardware, performance monitoring software will always yield more reliable results. Bottleneck calculators can't account for your installed drivers, background apps you should disable, or performance issues in the game you're benchmarking. A simple bottleneck percentage doesn't tell the full story.

TeraWulf Says AI Leasing Now Generates 83% of Revenue as Bitcoin Mining Recedes

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: TeraWulf’s November 6 earnings release shows $63 million in third-quarter high-performance-computing lease revenue, around 83% of its $76.2 million total. The company is increasingly an AI infrastructure business, although large non-cash gains and long-dated construction plans require careful reading.

TeraWulf is becoming a clear example of how the economics of Bitcoin mining can lead an infrastructure operator into a rather different business. In its third-quarter results published November 6, the Nasdaq-listed company said high-performance computing, or HPC, generated $63 million of lease revenue in the three months to September 30.

That represented about 83% of its $76.2 million quarterly revenue. Investors who still describe the firm primarily as a Bitcoin miner risk missing where its business is now concentrated and what its future costs may look like.

The company operates existing mining infrastructure at its Lake Mariner campus but is repurposing portions of that site to support contracted computing workloads. TeraWulf said 102 megawatts of revenue-generating critical IT capacity were online at Lake Mariner at quarter-end, with another 336 megawatts under construction.

These numbers are not interchangeable with power agreements or theoretical campus capacity. A contract for future electricity or land does not mean an equivalent amount of paying data-centre capacity is already operating. The distinction matters when a company is projecting revenue from facilities that require years and significant capital to complete.

TeraWulf also reported net income of approximately $709.9 million for the quarter. But it attributed a significant benefit to a non-cash change in the fair value of warrants, while adjusted EBITDA was negative at about $12.8 million. That makes the headline profit a poor substitute for analysing recurring rental revenue, financing costs and operating cash flow.

The company’s release described a 20-year Anthropic lease covering approximately 401 megawatts at its Justified development in Kentucky, representing around $19 billion in contracted revenue over the initial term. It also described a Google credit backstop supporting anticipated project financing and a definitive engineering, procurement and construction agreement with Fluor. These are material commercial commitments, not a claim that $19 billion has already been received.

TeraWulf expects phased service availability at Justified to begin in late 2027 and continue into 2028. At Muskie, it said contracted power capacity had expanded toward one gigawatt, subject to regulatory approval. Schedules, financing and utility infrastructure remain central risks.

NewsBTC has previously examined the mining industry’s move towards AI infrastructure. TeraWulf’s fresh results make that theme measurable: a majority of present revenue now comes from leases rather than mining.

A miner switching to AI can reduce its exposure to Bitcoin production economics, including changes in difficulty, coin price and power cost. It can also create demand for the same grid connections, facilities and financing that historically supported mining. That transition need not be immediately positive or negative for Bitcoin’s network; it depends on which machines are retired, which are redeployed and whether remaining miners take up capacity.

The contrast with NewsBTC’s discussion of rising network hashrate is telling. Competition for mining rewards can intensify even while some large listed companies reshape themselves into computing landlords.

The milestones to watch are completed megawatts, actual rental commencement, capital spending against guidance, contractual counterparty exposure and cash generation. TeraWulf’s results show a dramatic change in revenue mix, but the hardest part of its next chapter will be delivering enormous physical projects reliably and profitably.
